  - Decision, second layer: the adapter reads any `apiKeySource` but `none`, a value that is not a string included, as `Billing::ApiKey(source)`, kept once seen, and the executor turns it into `CredentialFinding::ApiKey`, which fails the run and points at managed settings. This is detection, not prevention: the finding is read when the run ends, so the run's work is refused but what it spent is spent. It is the one check that sees a key from outside the environment. Rejected: this check alone, blind to every routing above; reading Bedrock from the `model` string, undocumented and blind to a gateway; failing a run that reports no `apiKeySource`, which would fail every run of a release that drops the field.
  - What stays open: managed settings, set by an administrator of the machine, can still route a run through their `env` entry, undetected since it reports `none`; a run whose `init` lacks `apiKeySource` is not checked, so a release that drops the field silences the check until the fixtures are re-recorded (the contract test pins `repo_settings_ignored.jsonl` to `ApiKey("ANTHROPIC_API_KEY")`); the operator's own proxy and certificate settings, which agents inherit, are trusted.
- **Codex's own variables and interpreters' start-up code (OWL-125, decided 2026-10-02).** Checked on 2026-10-02 with codex-cli 0.154.0 installed with npm on macOS: `codex exec --skip-git-repo-check --ephemeral -s read-only "<prompt>"` under `env -i`, with a scratch `HOME` and `CODEX_HOME` per run holding a made-up `auth.json` (an API-key login, or a ChatGPT login whose tokens are an unsigned made-up JWT), every request sent to a dead proxy (`HTTPS_PROXY=http://127.0.0.1:9`) or to a local listener that recorded method, path, header names and JSON body keys only; each run stopped after 25 s.
  - `CODEX_REFRESH_TOKEN_URL_OVERRIDE` naming the listener, with an expired ChatGPT access token: Codex sent `POST /oauth/token` there, body keys `client_id`, `grant_type` and `refresh_token`. The login's refresh token goes to whatever host the variable names.
  - `CODEX_EXEC_SERVER_URL=ws://<listener>/exec`: Codex opened a websocket there, three `GET /exec` upgrade requests: a remote server to run the run's commands.
  - `CODEX_API_KEY`, with no login and a provider given on the command line (`-c model_provider=probe` with `base_url` naming the listener and `requires_openai_auth=true`, as only the runner could pass): every `POST /v1/responses` carried an `authorization` header; with no variable, with `OPENAI_API_KEY` or with `CODEX_ACCESS_TOKEN` there was none, and `OPENAI_ORGANIZATION` and `OPENAI_PROJECT` added no header.
  - `OPENAI_BASE_URL`, documented as the base URL of Codex's OpenAI provider, was ignored: with an API-key login and with a ChatGPT login the run still went to `wss://api.openai.com/v1/responses` or `wss://chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex/responses`. `CODEX_AUTHAPI_BASE_URL`, `CODEX_URL`, `CODEX_APP_SERVER_CHATGPT_BASE_URL` and `CODEX_OSS_BASE_URL` (without `--oss`) were not seen to send anything within 25 s. These results hold for 0.154.0, `exec` and the two logins tried.
  - The binary's strings name more of these, not tried: `CODEX_ACCESS_TOKEN` ("Agent Identity authentication"), `CODEX_REVOKE_TOKEN_URL_OVERRIDE`, `CODEX_CLOUD_TASKS_BASE_URL`, `CODEX_AGENT_IDENTITY_JWKS_BASE_URL`, `CODEX_CONNECTORS_TOKEN`, `CODEX_GITHUB_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN`, `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE`, `CODEX_SQLITE_HOME`, `OPENAI_IDENTITY_TOKEN_FILE`, `OPENAI_FEDERATION_RULE_ID`, `OPENAI_WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_CONTEXT`.
  - `NODE_OPTIONS="--require <probe.js>"`: `codex --version` ran the probe, since the npm install starts `bin/codex.js` under Node, which spawns the native binary with its own `process.env`; `claude --version` (2.1.284, a native binary) did not. `BASH_ENV` naming a file: `/bin/bash -c true` ran it, `/bin/sh -c true` (bash 3.2 as `sh`) ran neither it nor `ENV`'s, and neither did git running `GIT_SSH_COMMAND` (macOS only; dash, Linux's usual `sh`, reads `ENV` in interactive shells only).
  - Decision, Codex: `check_names` refuses, in any letter case, every name starting with `CODEX_` or `OPENAI_` (`HARNESS_VARIABLE_PREFIXES`, now pairing each prefix with its harness; `AgentEnvError::Harness { harness, name }`): "CODEX_EXEC_SERVER_URL cannot be passed to an agent: it is one of Codex's own variables (`CODEX_*`, `OPENAI_*`), which choose the server, login or token endpoint a Codex run uses, and agent runs keep those of the user's Codex configuration". The Claude Code message is unchanged. `CODEX_API_KEY` joins the floor's list and keeps the floor's refusal, as `OPENAI_API_KEY` does. A prefix for the reason OWL-120 gave: the names move between releases (`OPENAI_BASE_URL`, documented as routing, did not in 0.154.0) and the binary holds dozens; `OPENAI_` is refused as a guard on that history, not because a name under it was seen to route today. `CODEX_HOME` stays inherited, so a Codex run still finds the user's login, and declaring it is refused like any `CODEX_` name, which changes nothing since its value would be the same. Cost: a gate whose tests point an OpenAI SDK at a mock (`OPENAI_BASE_URL`, `OPENAI_API_VERSION`) can no longer declare those names, and an operator who sets `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE` relies on `SSL_CERT_FILE`, inherited and read by Codex too.
  - Decision, start-up code: `BASH_ENV` and `NODE_OPTIONS` are refused (`STARTUP_CODE_VARIABLES`, `AgentEnvError::StartupCode`): "NODE_OPTIONS cannot be passed to an agent: it makes an interpreter run code as it starts, in the harness or in a wrapper of git the runner starts outside the sandbox; set it in the gate command itself if the gate needs it". Two processes outside the sandbox get the agent environment: the sandbox program, and git, which the gate's checks (`Git::as_agent`) and `AgentEnv`'s lookup of the common git folder run as found on the agent's `PATH`, where it can be a bash wrapper (Nix's `makeWrapper`, an asdf or pyenv shim), and bash runs `BASH_ENV`'s file before git. Inside the sandbox, Codex's Node launcher would load `NODE_OPTIONS`'s code and could set any `CODEX_` name before it spawns the binary, which would undo the rule above. The dynamic-loader rule widens with the same reasoning: every `LD_` name, which glibc reads as its loader's, and `GCONV_PATH`, from which glibc loads code (`LOADER_PREFIXES`, `LOADER_VARIABLES`). Not refused: `PYTHONPATH`, `PYTHONSTARTUP`, `PERL5OPT`, `RUBYOPT` and `ENV`, which reach only interpreters that start inside the sandbox (the gate's and the agent's commands), where the code they load runs confined like the agent's own; a gate commonly needs `PYTHONPATH`. A gate that needs `NODE_OPTIONS` sets it in its command, `NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size=4096 npm test`, with a value the repository shows. This holds while no harness is written for another interpreter and the runner starts no other program outside the sandbox with the agent environment; either change reopens it. Rejected: refusing every interpreter's start-up variable, which costs `PYTHONPATH` for no process outside the sandbox; refusing none, which leaves the Codex rule one variable from undone.
  - What stays open: no Codex harness runs agents yet, so these rules apply from its adapter on. The user's own Codex configuration can name any variable as a provider's key (`env_key`) or header (`env_http_headers`), so a variable declared under such a name reaches it; whether Codex reads a repository's own `.codex/config.toml`, the equivalent of OWL-53 for Claude Code, was not checked. The binary also reads names outside these prefixes, not tried: OpenTelemetry's exporter settings (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` and `_HEADERS`) and `SSLKEYLOGFILE`. A Codex login set only through `CODEX_API_KEY` in the user's shell does not reach an agent run: the login must live in Codex's own configuration. OWL-130, below, checks these paths and Linux's shells.
- **Codex's remaining routing paths (OWL-130, decided 2026-10-02).** Checked on 2026-10-02 with codex-cli 0.154.0 installed with npm on macOS 26.6.2: `codex exec --ephemeral --json -s read-only "<prompt>"` in a fresh git repository, with an environment built from nothing but `PATH`, `TERM`, a scratch `HOME` and `CODEX_HOME` per run holding a made-up `auth.json` (an API-key login, or a ChatGPT login whose tokens are unsigned made-up JWTs) and the variables under test, under `sandbox-exec` with loopback network only. `HTTPS_PROXY` named a local proxy that recorded each `CONNECT` and refused it, or one that answered it with TLS under a throwaway certificate authority; local listeners recorded method, path, header names, JSON body keys and which planted made-up value appeared in a header or body, never a value. Each run stopped after 20 or 25 s. The user's own `~/.codex` was not read; the probe scripts were not kept.
  - A repository's `.codex/config.toml`: read only when the user's `config.toml` trusts the project (`[projects."<path>"] trust_level = "trusted"`). Untrusted, a file naming MCP servers, a provider, an exporter and `notify` changed nothing. Trusted, Codex ignored `model_provider`, `model_providers`, `openai_base_url`, `chatgpt_base_url`, `notify` and `otel` with the warning "Ignored unsupported project-local config keys … If you want these settings to apply, manually set them in your user-level config.toml", so a repository cannot name the provider or its base URL, but it loaded `mcp_servers`: an HTTP server received the MCP `initialize` request with an `Authorization` header holding the value of the variable its `bearer_token_env_var` names and a header holding the one `env_http_headers` names, and a stdio server's command ran as Codex started. A linked worktree of a trusted checkout and a subfolder of one were trusted; a trusted folder above the repository did not make it trusted. Agents run in worktrees of the runner's own clone (`ProjectDirs::checkout`), trusted only if the user marked that clone trusted, by running Codex there and accepting its prompt for instance.
  - An exec server: the binary's configuration fields hold none; `exec_server_url` in `config.toml` stopped the run under `--strict-config` ("unknown configuration field") and was ignored without it. `experimental_thread_store_endpoint` in a trusted repository's file made Codex refuse to start ("no longer supported"). A repository's `.env` was not read, trusted or not. `$CODEX_HOME/.env` was: its `HTTPS_PROXY` replaced the environment's, sending every request to the proxy it named, while its `CODEX_EXEC_SERVER_URL` opened no websocket.
  - The user's `config.toml`: a provider's `env_key` and `env_http_headers` sent the value of any variable they name, as `Authorization: Bearer` and as the named header, in every `POST /v1/responses`; a variable missing from the environment failed the run ("Missing environment variable"). The user's `mcp_servers` loaded in a run as a trusted repository's did, with the same headers, and a stdio server started.
  - `--ignore-user-config` ("Do not load `$CODEX_HOME/config.toml`; auth still uses `CODEX_HOME`"): the trusted repository's servers were not loaded and its command did not run, the user's servers, provider and exporter did not apply, and the login still did: with the throwaway authority trusted, the ChatGPT access token reached `chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex/responses` and the API key `api.openai.com/v1/responses`. `$CODEX_HOME/.env` was still read. A command-line override works only as an inline table: `-c 'projects={"<path>"={trust_level="untrusted"}}'`, naming the checkout or the worktree, left the servers unloaded, while the dotted form `-c 'projects."<path>".trust_level="untrusted"'`, with a path holding a dot, was silently not applied, and `-c 'projects={}'` cleared nothing.
  - OpenTelemetry: with no `[otel]` in the user's configuration, `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`, `_HEADERS` and the per-signal `_ENDPOINT` names sent nothing within 25 s. With `otlp-http` exporters for logs, traces and metrics in it, each with an endpoint and a header, Codex exported logs and traces there (no metrics within 25 s); the endpoint variables did not move them, but `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` added its header to every export. Neither the login nor a header value appeared in an exported body. Under `--ignore-user-config` nothing was exported.
  - `SSLKEYLOGFILE`: through the TLS proxy, with the throwaway authority trusted through `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE` or `SSL_CERT_FILE` (both honoured), the handshakes completed and the requests carried the login (websocket and HTTPS `responses`, models, plugins, analytics), and no key was written: with the API-key login the file was created and stayed empty, so some client of Codex opens it, and with the ChatGPT login it was not created.
  - Found on the way: under `--ignore-user-config` too, a ChatGPT login opens the ChatGPT apps server, `POST https://chatgpt.com/backend-api/ps/mcp` with the access token, and fetches the user's plugins (`/backend-api/ps/plugins/installed`). With `--disable apps` added, the apps server was not contacted; the plugins still were. Whether that server offers a model the connectors of the user's ChatGPT account was not tried.
  - Linux, `BASH_ENV` and `ENV`: no Linux host was at hand, so the check is a test that runs on the CI's ubuntu-latest and macos-latest legs on every pull request, rather than a line saying Linux was not checked: `git_started_for_the_agent_runs_no_shell_start_up_file` (`crates/owlshift-runner/src/executor/git.rs`). The runner's git, run with the agent environment as the gate's checks run it (`Git::as_agent`), through a `#!/bin/sh` wrapper and through a bash wrapper, with `ENV` declared and allowed and naming a file that leaves a mark, and `BASH_ENV` in the runner's environment, runs an alias through `sh -c` and leaves no mark. Controls in the same test: the bash wrapper run with `BASH_ENV` and `/bin/sh -i` with `ENV` leave it, so the mark works and bash reads `BASH_ENV` in a script, which is why it stays refused. It passed on 2026-10-02 on the CI's `ubuntu-24.04` image (version 20260927.320.1) and `macos-26-arm64` image (run 37024980246, pull request #112), and locally on macOS 26.6.2. The test does not check which shell `/bin/sh` is on the image (dash by Ubuntu's default, not checked here); it covers the shells git starts, not Codex on Linux.
  - Decision, configuration: the Codex adapter passes `--ignore-user-config` on every launch, as the Claude Code adapter passes `--setting-sources ""` (OWL-53). No project is then trusted, so a repository's `.codex/config.toml` and its MCP servers stay out whatever the user trusts, and the user's own MCP servers, providers with their variable mappings, and exporters stay out too; the login still comes from `CODEX_HOME`. It belongs in the adapter's guardrail arguments and is pinned by a unit test on its command line, as `GUARDRAIL_ARGS` is for Claude Code: an acceptance item of the Codex harness (P5), with a contract test that replays a trusted repository's MCP server. The OWL-125 message's last clause, "and agent runs keep those of the user's Codex configuration", now reads "and agent runs use Codex's own servers with the login of the user's Codex (`CODEX_HOME`)". Why the flag rather than the override: the override has to name the key trust resolves under, and its dotted form fails silently; the flag does not depend on how trust is keyed, and it also keeps the user's MCP servers, which can hold tracker and forge connectors, out of agent runs. Rejected: doing nothing, since a trusted checkout lets the ticket's branch, which agents write, start a program and send declared variables to a host it names. Cost: a user whose Codex goes through a provider or gateway of their own, or whose default model and settings live in `config.toml`, gets none of it in agent runs; agent runs use Codex's own servers, as a Claude Code agent run uses only the subscription token (OWL-120). The adapter sets what a run needs on its command line; with the user's configuration gone, the sandbox, approval and shell-environment settings are Codex's defaults unless it does.
  - Decision, variables: nothing new is refused, and this rests on the decision above. With the user's configuration ignored, no configuration maps a variable into a request and no exporter exists for `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` to add to; the endpoint variables did not route a run; `SSLKEYLOGFILE` wrote no key. A gate may still declare `OTEL_*` and `SSLKEYLOGFILE`. Either reopens if the adapter loads the user's configuration, or if a release writes keys to the file.
  - What stays open: paths other than Linux's shells were checked on macOS only, with Codex 0.154.0, `exec` and `-s read-only`; the adapter's own flags (sandbox mode, `exec resume`) were not tried with `--ignore-user-config`. `$CODEX_HOME/.env` is read whatever the flag and sets variables over the agent environment, so the Codex harness must keep `CODEX_HOME` out of an agent's write reach, or the run before could route the next. Not tried: hooks, rules, skills and plugins in `CODEX_HOME` or a trusted repository, and managed configuration (`requirements.toml`). The ChatGPT apps server above is the Codex harness's to settle.

- **For the executor (OWL-15).** In `owlshift_runner::agent_env`, `AgentEnv::from_runner` builds the environment and `AgentEnv::check` runs git and gh as the agent would, in the worktree. It reports credential variables (a token variable holding the placeholder passes), a password from `git credential fill`, a login from `gh auth token`, and the git settings that carry a credential the environment cannot remove: an http(s) URL with user info in a remote or a URL rewrite, an extra HTTP header, keys redacted. A probe that fails or times out counts as a finding. A finding stops the run before it starts; `AgentEnv::apply` then replaces the command's environment, and after the run `mcp_findings` fails a run whose `init` listed any MCP server. `floor::check_agent_environment` stays the check of declared names: on a whole agent environment it would flag the placeholder.
- **Tests.** `crates/owlshift-testkit/tests/agent_credentials.rs` plants token variables, a parent session's variables, a git credential helper, a gh login and repository settings carrying credentials on a host. The check finds each in the host's own environment and none in the agent environment, and a child spawned with it sees none of those variables. It runs the real git and gh on the three CI platforms, gh being required there. gh's first start on a fresh Windows runner took up to 14 s, past the probe's 10 s (OWL-56): every test binary whose probes run gh, this one and `on_demand.rs`, first starts it once through `owlshift_testkit::gh::warm_up`, which is why this test lives in the bench (OWL-70). The MCP half needs a harness login, which CI lacks: it rests on the adapter always passing the flags, unit tests on the `init` shapes above, and `crates/owlshift-runner/tests/agent_live.rs`, run by hand. On 2026-09-28 on the maintainer's machine (claude.ai connectors configured, gh login in the keyring, `osxkeychain` helper, SSH keys, launched from inside a Claude Code session) it passed: no credential finding, and the run completed on the subscription (`apiKeySource: none`) with no MCP server.
- **What stays open.** This closes what an agent reaches without going around Owlshift: its tool list, its environment, and git and gh as they run from its shell. A process that goes looking, by unsetting an override, reading a token file or querying the system keychain, is stopped by the operating system's sandbox of OWL-41, below. A harness configured through environment variables loses the ones it would inherit, as it already loses user settings to C1's flags. Claude Code's own (`ANTHROPIC_*`, `CLAUDE_*`, `CLAUDECODE`) cannot be put back either: since OWL-120 (above) they are refused as gate variables, declared or allowed, so no gateway (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`), Bedrock, Vertex or Foundry routing reaches a Claude Code run from the environment; the machine's managed settings still can. Any other variable reaches the harness only as a gate variable the project declares and the operator allows in `allow_gate_env` (OWL-63, OWL-75): a run has one agent environment, so a gate variable reaches the harness too. Reworded on 2026-10-02 (OWL-121): the previous text said such a harness loses its variables (a gateway `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, Bedrock or Vertex settings), which held only for inherited ones. From OWL-44 until OWL-120, a declared Claude Code variable off the credential floor, such as `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, did reach the harness: with no allowance until OWL-63, with the operator's after it. Native Windows was not run live (decision D9: WSL2 first).

**OWL-41 — confining agent runs: live checks.** Run on 2026-09-29 on the maintainer's machine (macOS 26.6.2, Apple silicon, Claude Code 2.1.284). The secrets were fake and made for the check: a gh-shaped `hosts.yml` and a `.env` planted in a scratch directory, and an item in a throwaway keychain file created with `security create-keychain`, named by its path in every call, never added to the search list, and deleted afterwards. The login keychain was not read. Claude Code's own login was only checked for presence, with `claude auth status`.

- Without confinement, `cat` reads the planted file and `security find-generic-password -s <item> -w <keychain file>` prints the fake secret. gh stores its login the same way, through the `security` program, and so does Claude Code (the binary runs `security find-generic-password -a <user> -w -s "Claude Code-credentials"`): any process of the user that runs `security` reads either one without a prompt.
- `/usr/bin/sandbox-exec` is present, although its manual page calls it deprecated. A profile applied with it cannot be stacked: `sandbox-exec` inside `sandbox-exec` fails with `sandbox_apply: Operation not permitted`.
- A confined process cannot exec a setuid binary (OWL-110). Checked on 2026-10-01 on the maintainer's machine (macOS 26.6.2): `/bin/ps` is setuid root (`-rwsr-xr-x root wheel`), and `sandbox-exec -p '(version 1)(allow default)' /bin/ps -o stat= -p <pid>` fails with `execvp() of '/bin/ps' failed: Operation not permitted` (exit 71), while the same command unconfined succeeds. Seatbelt refuses the exec whatever the profile allows, so this is a macOS limit, not a rule to relax. `/usr/bin/pgrep` is not setuid and is not affected. The first real `owlshift do` (OWL-107) stopped `blocked` on it: four CLI tests read a process's state with `ps`, so the gate could not pass in the sandbox. The tests now read the state with `sysctl` (`KERN_PROC_PID`) on macOS and from `/proc/<pid>/stat` on Linux, and run no `ps`. Any command a gate runs under confinement must not be setuid on macOS.
- A runner-applied profile of `(allow default)` plus denials works as follows:
  - `(deny file-read* (subpath <dir>))` makes `cat` fail with status 1.
  - Denying the keychain in either of two ways makes `security find-generic-password` fail at once, with status 44 ("could not be found") and no prompt: `(deny file-read* file-write*)` on the keychain files, or `(deny mach-lookup)` of `com.apple.SecurityServer` and `com.apple.securityd.xpc`. The client opens a keychain file itself.
  - The last matching rule wins. With the home denied and one folder allowed again (`file-read-data` and `file-read-xattr`), the folder's files can be read, `.env` and the token file cannot, listing the home fails, and `stat` and `pwd -P` still work.
  - Corrected on 2026-09-29, checked live the same day. The last matching rule wins only among rules naming the same operations. An operation named (`file-read-data`) outranks the wildcard (`file-read*`), whatever the order. A later `(allow file-read* …)` does not reopen a folder an earlier `(deny file-read-data …)` closed: a file in it could be neither listed nor read. A later `(deny file-read* …)` does not close a file inside a folder an earlier `(allow file-read-data …)` opened: a planted credential file in an opened folder was read. The `pwd -P` above was the shell's builtin, which answers from `$PWD`. The programs `/bin/pwd -P`, Python's `os.getcwd()` and git fail with "Operation not permitted" in a folder whose parent is closed, since `getcwd` lists each folder above the working directory. Allowing `file-read-data` on each such parent alone (`literal`: its names, not its files) lets them work.
  - Such a profile gives no write restriction of its own: a write into the home succeeded.
- Claude Code cannot log in once the keychain is denied: under the keychain-file denial, `claude auth status` reports `loggedIn: false`, `authMethod: none`. It reads a plain-text login instead: with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` naming a scratch directory whose `.credentials.json` held a made-up `claudeAiOauth` record, it reported `loggedIn: true`, `authMethod: claude.ai`. The binary names this store "Storing credentials in plaintext" and keeps it as a fallback to the keychain. Writing a new login there, through `claude auth login` under the same profile, needs a browser and was not tried. Tried on 2026-09-30, it fails: see OWL-94 below.
- Claude Code's own sandbox (`sandbox.enabled`, 2.1.284) covers the Bash tool only; the CLI's own process and its file tools stay outside it. Three tiny `claude -p` runs on `haiku` at effort `low` checked it, each with the adapter's flags, under `env -i HOME PATH USER LANG TMPDIR`, and with `--settings` setting `failIfUnavailable: true`, `allowUnsandboxedCommands: false`, `autoAllowBashIfSandboxed: true` and a `filesystem.denyRead` list. Each run was asked to `cat` the planted file and to run the `security` read, retrying a denied command once with `dangerouslyDisableSandbox`:
  - With `denyRead` naming the file's directory: `cat` was denied, retry included, while the `security` read printed the fake secret. The profile the binary generates allows `mach-lookup` of `com.apple.SecurityServer` and `com.apple.securityd.xpc` ("Specific mach-lookup permissions for security operations").
  - With the keychain directories added to `denyRead`: both commands failed, retries included, with status 1 and status 44. The run completed on the subscription login (`apiKeySource: none`).
  - With the same `--settings` and a `.claude/settings.local.json` in the run's (untrusted) directory adding `sandbox.filesystem.allowRead` for the keychain directory: the `security` read printed the fake secret. A settings file an agent can write widens the sandbox that `--settings` set.

Linux and native Windows go through the CI matrix, since only macOS is a local host (decision D9, D12). The Linux checks below ran on `ubuntu-latest` in a throwaway workflow on the OWL-41 branch on 2026-09-28 ([run 36494641520](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/36494641520)); the workflow is removed before the pull request. The image was Ubuntu 24.04.5 (kernel 6.17-azure), LSMs `lockdown,capability,landlock,yama,apparmor,ima,evm`. All secrets were fake.

- The unconfined baseline read the planted gh token file, a `.env` in a sibling directory, and committed in a linked worktree: exit 0 throughout, as on macOS.
- `kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns = 1` on the image. A plain `unshare -Ur` fails (`write /proc/self/uid_map: Operation not permitted`), and `bwrap` is not installed. Installed from the archive (bubblewrap 0.9.0), it still fails: `bwrap: setting up uid map: Permission denied`, because the package shipped no AppArmor profile granting user namespaces and the restriction blocks them. So on a stock 24.04, bwrap needs a one-time privileged setup.
- With an AppArmor profile that grants `bwrap` the `userns` capability loaded (`apparmor_parser -r`, needs root), bwrap confines correctly: the run reads the allowed toolchain folder (exit 0) but not the planted token file or the sibling `.env` (exit 1 each), a session-bus connect is refused (exit 1, `/run/user` mounted as a fresh tmpfs), and it writes and commits in the bind-mounted worktree (exit 0). `bwrap` keeps the child in the host PID namespace (no `--unshare-pid`), so the runner's process-group kill still reaps the tree: a `setsid` process group holding a bwrap and two `sleep`s was fully killed by one group `SIGKILL`.
- Landlock (ABI 7, no privilege and no user namespace, so it works under the AppArmor restriction) confines the file system as well: the token file and the sibling `.env` are refused (exit 1), the home cannot be listed (exit 2), a write to the home is refused, and the worktree stays writable and committable. Its limit: connecting to the Secret Service's Unix socket still succeeded (exit 0), since Landlock gates file-system access rights, not `connect`, and offers no network restriction. bwrap closes that path by mounting `/run/user` empty.
- `bwrap --unshare-pid --proc /proc` was checked separately ([run 36496118050](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/36496118050), same branch, removed before the pull request), because without it the read-only bind of `/proc` leaves every same-user host process visible and its `/proc/<pid>/environ` readable — the runner's own credentials among them. With it, a confined process saw only its own five PIDs, could not see a marker host process, and its read of that process's `environ` failed (exit 1). The host process group still reaped the whole tree: a `setsid` group holding two `bwrap` processes, a shell and two `sleep`s was gone after one group `SIGKILL`, so `--unshare-pid` does not break the runner's process-tree stop. The runner therefore runs bwrap with `--unshare-pid --proc /proc --die-with-parent`, and without `--unshare-net` (the harness needs the network).

Decision (2026-09-29): the runner wraps every agent run and every gate command in an OS sandbox it builds itself — `sandbox-exec` with a generated Seatbelt profile on macOS, `bwrap` on Linux — around the harness and everything it starts (approach A, chosen by the maintainer). The home is unreadable but for an allow-list (worktree, the repository's git directory, the harness's login and install folders, tool-chain folders), writes are confined to the worktree, the repository's git directory and the run's own temporary folder, and the system credential store is closed (the Keychain on macOS, `/run/user` on Linux). Because that closes the Keychain to Claude Code too, an agent run authenticates through a login of its own. Decided first as a second login of the same account, made once with `claude auth login` inside the sandbox in a folder `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` names; that login cannot be made on macOS, and OWL-94 (below) replaced it with a `claude setup-token` token the runner hands the harness. Residual risk, recorded: an agent can read that one token, and the maintainer can revoke it; on Linux a missing or user-namespace-blocked `bwrap` refuses the run, and `owlshift doctor` reports how to fix it; native Windows fails closed and points to WSL2 (decision D9). Landlock was evaluated as a Linux alternative — it needs no privilege and survives the AppArmor restriction — but it cannot hide the Secret Service socket or restrict the network, so bwrap is the default and Landlock is left as a possible fallback.

- **As built.** `owlshift_platform::sandbox` turns a program and its arguments into the wrapped command, from a `Policy` of plain paths, each made real first (Seatbelt compares real paths: `/tmp` is `/private/tmp`). On macOS the profile is given inline with `-p` and every path as a `-D` parameter read with `(param …)`, checked live on 2026-09-29 with a path holding spaces, parentheses and a double quote: nothing is quoted into the profile, and no profile file exists for a command to rewrite. Its rules go from the widest to the narrowest, the last match winning. Every read rule names `file-read-data file-read-xattr`, never the wildcard, per the correction above. The order is:
  1. Everything allowed.
  2. No write but `/dev`.
  3. The home and the closed folders neither read nor written.
  4. The readable, writable and temporary folders opened again.
  5. Each folder above an opened one listable by itself, for `getcwd`.
  6. The protected paths not written.
  7. The hidden paths and the Keychain files neither read, `stat`ed nor written.
  8. The security daemons' lookups denied.

  On Linux, bwrap, found in the system folders only, runs with `--die-with-parent --unshare-pid --ro-bind / / --dev /dev --proc /proc`, a private `/tmp`, and empty `/run/user`, `/var/tmp`, home and closed folders. The policy's folders are then bound back, the shallowest first; a hidden file inside a bound folder is replaced by `/dev/null`. Arguments pass after `--` as they are.
- **The policy** (`owlshift_runner::agent_env::AgentEnv::policy`). Read in the home: git's configuration (`~/.gitconfig`, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/git`), the tool chains (`~/.rustup`, `~/.cargo/bin`, `~/.cargo/registry`, `~/.cargo/git`) and the folders the agent's `PATH` and declared variables name under the home, by the rule of OWL-68 below, never the home itself; the harness's install folders. Written: the worktree, the repository's common git folder but for its `hooks`, `config`, `config.worktree` and `info`, and the run's own temporary folder, which holds the harness's configuration folder for the run (OWL-94).
  - The executor makes that folder per run, 0700, under the system temporary folder, and removes it when the run returns. `TMPDIR`, `TMP` and `TEMP` name it inside the sandbox. It is the only temporary folder written.
  - An inherited `TMPDIR` is never trusted. The temporary folders the runner inherited (`TMPDIR`, `TMP`, `TEMP`, the system's) and the shared `/tmp` and `/var/tmp` are closed, neither read nor written: every process of the user shares them, other tools' files and other sessions' scratch folders included.
  - On Linux `/tmp` is private to the sandbox anyway.
  - On macOS, Apple's developer shims in `/usr/bin` (git, clang, make) still print `error: couldn't create cache file '…/T/xcrun_db-…'`. They write their cache to the per-user temporary folder whatever `TMPDIR` says, and `xcrun_nocache` does not stop the write (checked on 2026-09-29). The command still runs (git exited 0). The folder stays closed: opening those cache files would let an agent rewrite where the user's own `xcrun` finds its tools. Hidden wherever they are: `.ssh`, `.aws`, `.azure`, `.kube`, `.docker`, `.gnupg`, `.netrc`, `.npmrc`, `.pypirc`, `.git-credentials`, `.config/gh`, `.config/gcloud`, the cargo and git credential files, `.local/share/keyrings`, Maven's `settings.xml` and `settings-security.xml`, Gradle's `gradle.properties`, `Library/Keychains`, and the caller's run folder, which holds the logs. A project whose gate needs more of the home, such as another tool chain, declares a variable naming it (OWL-68, below).
- **Declared tool-chain paths (OWL-68).** Decision (2026-09-29): a folder that a declared variable's value names under the home is opened, read-only, when the agent is confined, so `JAVA_HOME=~/.sdkman/candidates/java/current` reaches the gate as a name, a value and a readable folder. The value is split like `PATH` (`std::env::split_paths`), and every variable of the declared name is read, in any letter case. The same rule, `named_folder`, now also judges the `PATH`'s folders, which were compared as written: `~/bin/..` or a link to the home opened the whole home. An entry is judged by its real path (`fs::canonicalize`, which resolves `..`, links and, on macOS, letter case), and that real path is what the sandbox opens. Nothing is opened for:
  - a relative or missing path, or a file: a URL splits into pieces that are no folder;
  - the home or a folder above it;
  - a folder outside the home: the sandbox already leaves it readable or closes it on purpose;
  - a folder that is, lies in or holds a path the run hides, closes or writes in the home: the credential paths above, git's `credentials`, the run's hidden paths, an inherited `TMPDIR` under the home, the worktree and the run's temporary folder.

  Holding a hidden path keeps a broad folder closed: `~/.config` holds `.config/gh` and may hold credentials no list names, and `~/Library` holds the Keychain. Lying in one matters on Linux, where bwrap mounts by depth, so a readable folder inside a hidden one would be mounted over it. Keeping out the folders the agent writes means a link the agent could swap is never judged. A refused entry is left out silently, and the gate's own tool names the path it cannot read. The guarantee is about known paths only: an allowed variable naming `~/Projects` opens every other project's files, `.env` included, so the operator allows names whose values are tool chains. Maven's and Gradle's credential files join the hidden list with this change, since `GRADLE_USER_HOME` or `~/.m2` are what a JVM gate would declare; as a result, a declared `~/.gradle` holding `gradle.properties` is refused whole, and the variable should name the tool chain's own folder. When the value names its folder through a link in the home, such as sdkman's `current`, the sandbox recreates that link where it is named (`Policy::links`, `bwrap --symlink`): bwrap's home starts empty. macOS needs nothing. Checked live on 2026-09-29 with `sandbox-exec` on the maintainer's machine: a home closed and one folder in it reopened, `cat` through an absolute link, a relative link and a `..` path in the closed home all read the reopened folder (exit 0). Checked the same day with `/bin/realpath`: `home/.SSH` resolves to `Home/.ssh` on APFS, so a letter-case variant is judged as the path it opens. Tests: `the_policy_opens_named_folders_by_their_real_path_and_never_a_credential` in `agent_env.rs` covers each rule on a real temporary home. `a_confined_command_reads_a_declared_tool_chain_and_no_credential_folder` runs `cat` in the real sandbox, as the gate is spawned. It reads a tool chain through sdkman's `current` link, and reads nothing in a declared `~/.config` or `~/.aws/bin`. The sandbox's unit test pins the `--symlink` argument. Both passed on macOS on 2026-09-29; the Linux leg of CI runs them with confinement required.
- **In the executor.** An `AgentEnv` is confined from `new` and `from_runner`, and carries no login: the harness's is set on the harness command alone (OWL-94, below). `Executor::run` first asks `sandbox_ready`: where agents cannot be confined, nothing is written and the run is refused (`ExecutorError::Spawn`, whose message names the fix, WSL2 on Windows). OWL-22's probes then run inside the sandbox, so a finding means a credential the agent could still reach. The harness says what it needs (`Harness::sandbox_needs`, nothing by default): Claude Code its install folders, read, and its login, a token (OWL-94, below); a confined run without one is refused with the fix. The harness command and each gate command are spawned as `AgentEnv::confine` returns them, with the agent's variables; the gate gets the run's paths without the harness's folders. A project may not declare an `LD_` or `DYLD_` name, or `GCONV_PATH` (OWL-125), for its gate: the sandbox program would load it before the sandbox applies. The only way to run bare is `AgentEnv::without_confinement`, compiled for tests and behind the `testkit` feature, which only the test bench enables; a test checks that the CLI's code never names it.
- **The one-time login, superseded.** The agent login was first made once with `claude auth login` under `sandbox-exec` with the Keychain's daemons denied (plain `claude auth login` on Linux), `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` naming `<Owlshift's configuration folder>/agent-login/claude`, on the assumption that the CLI, finding no Keychain, would write its plain-text store there. It does not: see OWL-94, below, which replaced it on every platform.
- **Tests.** `sandbox`'s unit tests pin the Seatbelt profile and the bwrap arguments on every platform, a path that is not UTF-8 refused rather than mangled, and native Windows refused with WSL2 named. `executor::gate`'s `a_confined_gate_command_reaches_no_planted_secret` plants fake secrets: a gh token file and another project's `.env` in a fake home, a file in a folder the runner's inherited `TMPDIR` names, and a file in the shared temporary folder. A bare control reads each; confined gate commands do not, within a deadline, so a prompt would fail the test rather than pass it. In git's opened configuration folder, the configuration is read and a planted `credentials` file is not. A write into the home or into the inherited `TMPDIR` never lands. The worktree stays writable, the run writes in its own temporary folder, which `TMPDIR` names, and `/bin/pwd -P` works in the worktree under its closed parent; on Linux the runner's own process and `/run/user/<uid>` are out of sight; on a macOS CI runner, the item of a throwaway keychain (created unlocked, never on the search list, deleted afterwards) is out of reach, a check kept off the maintainer's machine, where no automated run may risk a Keychain dialog. `tests/confinement.rs` in the test bench runs a harness that is a shell script through `Executor::run`: neither it nor its child reads the planted files, and a grandchild left behind is stopped with the run. The scenarios run bare, since the isolation scenarios test the isolation check, which the sandbox would pre-empt; `smoke_confined` plays the smoke scenario confined, a legitimate build committed and pushed. Where agents cannot be confined these tests say so and are skipped, unless `OWLSHIFT_REQUIRE_CONFINEMENT` is set. Run on 2026-09-29 on the maintainer's macOS machine: every one passed with the real sandbox.
- **CI.** The ubuntu leg installs bubblewrap and loads a bwrap-scoped AppArmor profile, `.github/apparmor/bwrap`; the runner-wide sysctl is left as it is. It sets `OWLSHIFT_REQUIRE_CONFINEMENT=1`, so a confinement test that cannot run fails there instead of skipping. This changes CI settings, which the floor of architecture section 8 says needs an explicit human approval: the maintainer approved it on OWL-41 on 2026-09-29. On 2026-09-29 ([run 36535693787](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/36535693787)) ubuntu, macOS and Windows passed:
  - On ubuntu the confinement tests ran rather than skipped: the test step ran with the variable set, and the executor test took 6.18 s, its waits included.
  - On macOS they ran with the real sandbox, the keychain check included.
  - On Windows both refusals were checked.

**OWL-241 — CI time: no on_demand on Windows, the tests under cargo-nextest (decided 2026-10-12).** CI's slowest leg went from 3 to 29 minutes in two weeks, because of one test binary: `crates/owlshift-testkit/tests/on_demand.rs` holds every `do` behind the process-wide `RUNS` mutex, so its tests run one after the other, and the Windows leg ran it twice (OWL-81's separate step, then `cargo test --workspace`). Decision, approved by the maintainer on 2026-10-12 as the CI-settings floor of architecture section 8 requires:

- **No on_demand on the Windows leg.** Those tests run `do` without confinement, which a native Windows user cannot reach: `do` refuses there and WSL2 is the supported path (D9, OWL-87). The refusals run outside that binary and stay on the Windows leg: `native_windows_refuses_to_confine` (`owlshift-platform/src/sandbox.rs`), `a_confined_gate_command_is_refused_on_native_windows` (`owlshift-runner/src/executor/gate.rs`), `a_run_on_native_windows_is_refused` (`owlshift-testkit/tests/confinement.rs`). The leg keeps format, lint, rustdoc, doctests and every other test, so it still proves the workspace builds natively (D2). The filter, `-E 'not binary_id(owlshift-testkit::on_demand)'`, stands in the workflow.
- **The tests run under cargo-nextest**, one process per test, `--no-fail-fast` and without retries: a child forked by one test no longer holds another test's lock, and on_demand's tests use the runner's cores. nextest runs no doctests, so `cargo test --workspace --doc` keeps a step. `cargo test --workspace` still works locally and stays `owlshift.toml`'s gate, which a developer's machine runs without nextest; the `RUNS` mutex stays for it, and `the_repositorys_own_project_file_gates_on_what_ci_runs` (`owlshift-cli/tests/cli.rs`) maps that gate to CI's two steps.
- **OWL-81's step is retired.** It ran on_demand alone on Windows, where gh's first start on a fresh machine is slow (OWL-56), to prove that binary passes without another having warmed gh. With on_demand off that leg, the check goes too; nothing else replaces it, since the slow start is the machine's (reading gh from disk), which no test process can reset. `gh::warm_up` stays as it is: once per process, so under nextest each test process starts gh (`gh --version`, about 0.1 s when warm) before its probes run (`on_demand.rs`, `Bench`; `agent_credentials.rs`, which still runs on Windows).
- **A third-party action.** nextest 0.9.146 (2026-09-21) is installed by `taiki-e/install-action` pinned to its full commit `f7e5d7c961414b23f5b25b2da9294395d08513ad` (v2.87.26, 2026-10-06), which downloads the prebuilt release and checks its checksum. The maintainer approved this dependency with the change; the workflow's permissions are unchanged.
- **Measured on 2026-10-12.** Locally (macOS, Apple silicon): on_demand's 69 tests took 192 s under `cargo test` and 32 s under nextest; the workspace, 640 tests, 42 s. In CI, two runs of `main` before the change ([38279848629](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/38279848629), [38279797034](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/38279797034)) against two attempts of [run 38288616965](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/38288616965), the change merged into `main` as it then stood, on_demand three tests larger (72). Before, on_demand was the `cargo test` binary's time; after, the summed time of its tests and the wall time of the whole test run:

  | Leg | Job before | Job after | on_demand before | on_demand after |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | ubuntu | 5.0 / 5.9 min | 3.1 / 4.0 min | 139 / 145 s | 164 / 182 s of test time, within a 65 / 69 s test run |
  | macOS | 16.1 / 15.3 min | 8.5 / 7.4 min | 703 / 657 s | 800 / 754 s of test time, within a 359 / 341 s test run |
  | Windows | 29.3 / 25.5 min | 5.4 / 5.8 min | 701 + 664 s / 611 + 572 s | not run |

  Under nextest on_demand's tests overlap the rest and spread over the runner's cores; the slowest single test took 7 s on ubuntu and 32 s on macOS. On Windows the three refusals passed in both attempts. nextest reports one test as leaky on Windows, `drive_does_not_wait_for_a_process_holding_the_output_open`, whose point is a process left holding the output open; it passes.

**OWL-289 — CI time: the Rust build is cached (decided 2026-10-15).** Every leg recompiled the workspace's dependencies on every run, and clippy only checks, so the Test step compiled them again. Decision, approved by the maintainer on 2026-10-15 as the CI-settings floor of architecture section 8 requires (recorded on the issue):

- **The action.** `Swatinem/rust-cache` v2.9.2, pinned to its full commit `6323deb102c322ba6fcbdcafc7e3dddab59af2b6` (tag checked live on 2026-10-15 with `git ls-remote`: `refs/tags/v2.9.2^{}`), on the single `check` job, so on the three legs, after the toolchain install: the key holds the rustc versions, which must be known by then. The workflow's permissions are unchanged.
- **Default settings.** The maintainer chose them over `save-if` on `main` only. A run whose key is not cached saves it (when its job succeeds); a pull request whose Cargo files and toolchain match `main`'s restores `main`'s exact key and saves nothing. The key (v2.9.2's README) holds the job id, the OS and architecture, every installed rustc, and a hash of all `Cargo.lock`, `Cargo.toml`, `rust-toolchain.toml` and `.cargo/config.toml` files, so any change to one of them is a cache miss, which restores the closest older key and saves a new cache. A pull request reads `main`'s caches and its own, never another branch's. Only dependencies are cached: the workspace's own crates are rebuilt every run. The action sets `CARGO_INCREMENTAL=0` for the job, so the times below are not the cache alone. `~/.cargo/bin` is cached too (`cache-bin` is on).
- **Cost.** The caches are 396 MB (ubuntu), 211 MB (macOS) and 214 MB (Windows) per key (the sizes the save logged); the repository's cache quota is 10 GB and the oldest caches are evicted past it. Saving added 8 / 16 / 20 s to the cold run's job. Every pull request that changes a Cargo file saves three new caches.
- **Measured on 2026-10-15** on [run 38694993648](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/38694993648) of this change's pull request, attempt 1 (cold: no cache existed) and attempt 3 (warm: re-run of the same commit, restored by exact key on the three legs; `Cache restored successfully`, `full match: true`), against [run 38692727855](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/38692727855) of `main` the same morning (no cache) and [run 38631426767](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/38631426767) (Test only: 3 min 08 s, 10 min 34 s, 4 min 09 s). Job time / Test step, from the jobs' step timestamps:

  | Leg | `main` | Cold | Warm |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | ubuntu | 4:35 / 3:08 | 4:25 / 2:50 | 3:17 / 2:17 |
  | macOS | 11:42 / 10:29 | 11:37 / 10:01 | 11:08 / 10:13 |
  | Windows | 5:57 / 3:50 | 5:15 / 3:11 | 3:21 / 1:54 |

  The Lint step went, cold to warm, from 33 / 46 / 54 s to 11 / 15 / 29 s on ubuntu / macOS / Windows. Attempt 2 of the same run, warm on ubuntu and Windows (2:45 / 2:01 and 4:43 / 2:58), shows the spread between two runs of one leg: about a minute on a job, so the table's differences of under a minute are not a result.
- **What it buys.** The ubuntu and Windows jobs lose about 1.1 and 1.9 min once warm. macOS, the longest leg and so what a pull request waits for, loses about 0.5 min: its Test step is the nextest run itself (on_demand's tests, 10 min), not compilation, which no build cache shortens. The next saving on that leg is on_demand's own time, a separate issue.
- **A miss that is not the code's.** Attempt 2's macOS leg found no cache (`No cache found.`) and saved a new one: the pool of `macos-latest` runners mixes two image versions (`20260907.0351` in attempts 1 and 3, `20261013.0424` in attempt 2), and the newer one has a second rustc (1.99.0) installed, which is part of the key (`...-5976a727` against `...-f0fdd15e`). Until the pool holds one image, a macOS run can miss and pay for a cold build and a save; it did not change a result (that attempt's macOS job took 12:43, Test 10:53, the slowest of the four). Making the key ignore the image's toolchains takes a setting other than the default, which the maintainer did not choose.
- **First effect on `main`.** `main` has no cache until its first run after this change merges and saves one; a pull request reads it from then on. Until then a pull request starts cold, as attempt 1 did.

**OWL-94 — the agent login is a `claude setup-token` token: live check.** The one-time login above fails on macOS. Checked on 2026-09-30 on the maintainer's machine (macOS 26.6.2, Claude Code 2.1.284) with the exact command `owlshift doctor` printed: the browser OAuth succeeds, then the CLI stops with "Login failed: Couldn't save your login. If your Mac's keychain is locked, unlock it and log in again." It writes only `.claude.json` in the folder, no `.credentials.json`, so doctor kept failing and every `do` was refused.

The replacement was checked the same day, before any code, with a real token made by `claude setup-token` ("Set up a long-lived authentication token (requires Claude subscription)"). The confined commands came from Owlshift's own code: `AgentEnv::from_runner` and `AgentEnv::confine`, so the real Seatbelt profile (home closed, Keychain files and daemons denied) and the real agent environment, with the adapter's exact `claude -p` flags. `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` named an empty folder. The token reached the confined process through its environment only, and the script printed exit codes and fixed fields, never the token.

- Control, no token: `claude auth status` exits 1, `loggedIn: false`, `authMethod: "none"`.
- With the token: `claude auth status` exits 0, `loggedIn: true`, `authMethod: "oauth_token"`. It does not check the token: a made-up one gets the same answer. So doctor can only look for the token, not tell whether it still works.
- With the token, `claude -p` with the read-only flags, `haiku` at effort `low`: exit 0; `init` says `apiKeySource: "none"` and no MCP server; the result is `subtype: "success"`, `is_error: false`, `terminal_reason: "completed"`, and the reply was the one asked for. With a made-up token the same run gets API status 401. The run completed on the subscription.
- Whether the agent's own shell commands see the token: the run asking for it completed, but its answer was unclear. Taken as yes, the safe reading. Settled by OWL-96 (below): their environment did not hold it, but the exec block of the `claude` process did, and they could read that; since OWL-96 the token reaches the CLI on a descriptor and is in no exec block.
- The configuration folder fills during a run: `.claude.json`, `backups`, `projects`, `sessions`, `session-env`, `shell-snapshots`, `telemetry`, `policy-limits.json`, `remote-settings.json` and their locks. No file there named `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` or held an `sk-ant-` string (counted with `grep -l`, values never printed).
- Linux, unchecked on 2026-09-30: there was no host here and no login in CI. Checked on 2026-10-08 in a Linux container by OWL-101 (OWL-96, below): a real token logs a confined `claude -p` in on the subscription, handed over on the descriptor since OWL-96. Not checked: a bare-metal Linux host.

Decision (2026-09-30, OWL-94): on every platform, a confined agent run logs in to Claude Code with a long-lived token of the operator's subscription, made by `claude setup-token`.

- **Where it lives.** `owlshift init` stores it in the system keychain, service `owlshift`, account `claude-agent`, like the Linear key and the GitHub token (`init::CLAUDE_TOKEN`). The runner reads it in `owlshift do`, with the other secrets. The agent never reads the keychain, which the sandbox closes.
- **How it reaches the harness.** The Claude Code harness holds it (`ClaudeHarness::login`) and declares it in `Harness::sandbox_needs` as a `HarnessLogin`. The executor gives it to the harness command alone, right before the spawn (`HarnessLogin::apply`): first as `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`, and since OWL-96 on a pipe the command inherits, named by `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN_FILE_DESCRIPTOR` (below). It is never in `AgentEnv`'s variables, which the ticket had put in `from_runner`: kept out, it never reaches a gate command or the credential probes of OWL-22. Those probes still flag every Claude or Anthropic variable, and both variables are on the floor's list, so a project cannot declare them.
- **A configuration folder of the run's own.** The executor also sets `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` to an empty folder, `harness-config`, inside the run's own temporary folder, which is private to the user and removed with the run. The runner makes it and binds it read and written into the sandbox: bwrap makes the temporary folder anew, empty, in its private `/tmp`, so a folder made there beforehand would not be seen (the first Linux CI run showed it). The CLI finds no other login there, as the check above ran, and nothing it writes outlives the run: transcripts, shell snapshots and settings stay with the run. A settings file one run wrote cannot reach the next. Rejected: one folder per install, `<config>/agent-login/claude`, which the ticket named. The runs of every project would share it and write into it, and a machine that made the old login could still hold a plain-text refresh token there for every agent to read. `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is no longer inherited from the runner, and `paths::claude_agent_login_dir` is gone.
- **Refusals.** A confined Claude Code run without a stored token, or with one that is blank or holds a space or a control character, is refused before anything is cloned (`NoAgentLogin`). The fix: run `claude setup-token`, then `owlshift init` and paste the token it printed; `owlshift init --replace-secrets` replaces a stored token that expired, was revoked or was pasted wrong. A Claude Code failure with API status 401 names the same fix. `init` refuses to store any secret that is not one word, as one pasted across lines is.
- **What never holds the token.** Events, briefs and error messages never hold it: the `Secret` type prints `<redacted>` in debug output, and no refusal names a value. The run's log files hold `<redacted>` where the harness printed it, even split across two writes (`RunLog`). A `result.json` or an artifact that holds it fails the run with a credential finding that names the file, never the value: what they say would reach the tracker, the pull request and the events.
- **Residual risk, recorded.** Until OWL-96, the agent could read the token: Claude Code drops `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` from its tools' environment, but the `claude` process was started with it, and its exec block stays readable by the same user (inside the macOS sandbox through `sysctl(KERN_PROCARGS2)`, checked by OWL-96; on Linux through `/proc/<pid>/environ`). Since OWL-96 the token reaches the CLI on a pipe, and is in no exec block. What remains:
  - The guarantee rests on the CLI reading the pipe at start-up, before any tool runs, as Claude Code 2.1.284 does (checked with a made-up token, below). A process the harness started before that read, or a CLI that ignored the variable, would find the token in the pipe: Owlshift consumes nothing itself, it only never writes the token elsewhere.
  - The token stays in the `claude` process's memory. Reading it takes a debugger's access to that process (`ptrace` on Linux, `task_for_pid` on macOS), which the sandbox does not deny; the host's own rules decide (Yama's `ptrace_scope`, the binary's hardened runtime). Not checked.
  - On macOS, which has no `pipe2`, the pipe is close-on-exec a moment after it is made. It is made while no process tree can start, so no harness or gate command is forked then; a process another runner thread started with a bare spawn at that moment could inherit it. The runner starts none alongside a harness today.
  - The network stays open, so a harness or process that got the token could send it anywhere, or copy it into a file of the worktree or a commit, which no check reads. The operator revokes the token like any Claude Code login. `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` closes none of this (OWL-96).
- **Tests.**
  - Unit tests: `init`'s new secret on the in-memory keychain, and the refusal of one pasted wrong; doctor's check, whether stored, absent or unreadable; the harness's refusals and its login; the log's redaction, the token split across writes included.
  - The bench's `a_confined_harness_logs_in_with_its_token_and_no_output_keeps_it`, in `tests/confinement.rs`, runs a shell-script harness confined through `Executor::run`. It sees its token and an empty configuration folder under the run's temporary folder, which is gone after the run. It prints its whole environment and the token, yet no file of the run directory and not the report holds it; a result holding it fails the run. It passed on the maintainer's macOS machine on 2026-09-30. Since OWL-96 it reads the token from the descriptor it is named, and its probe also checks what OWL-96 settles (below).
  - The live tests `agent_live` and `executor_live` read the token from the keychain and run confined.

**OWL-96 — can the agent's shell read the token: live check.** Checked on 2026-09-30 on the maintainer's machine (macOS 26.6.2, Claude Code 2.1.284) with a made-up token, never the real one. A shell command needs a model turn, and a made-up token gets none from the real API, so `claude -p` was pointed (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`) at a local fake Messages API that answered with scripted tool calls: a `Bash` probe, a `Write` of `written.txt`, and a `Bash` write. The fake API confirmed each request carried the made-up token. The probe printed presence and counts, never a value. The confined runs used Owlshift's own `AgentEnv::from_runner`, `ClaudeHarness::sandbox_needs` and `AgentEnv::confine` (the real Seatbelt profile), the adapter's write-in-worktree flags, and an empty `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`.

- What `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` is, read in the 2.1.284 binary: a hardening mode for runs on untrusted input, such as a GitHub Actions workflow. With it set to `1`, the CLI forces the permission mode to `default`, whatever `--permission-mode` asks, and says so on standard error: "Permission mode forced to default — CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB is set (allowed_non_write_users hardening)".
- The token in the tools' environment: absent in every run, scrub or not. The CLI drops `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` from its subprocesses by itself.
- The token in a process's exec block: unconfined, `ps eww` found it in one process, the `claude` one. Confined, `ps` is refused ("operation not permitted"), but a script calling `sysctl(KERN_PROCARGS2)` read the exec blocks of about 700 processes and found the token in one, with or without the scrub.
- The scrub's cost: under `--permission-mode acceptEdits`, the `Write` call was denied ("Permission for this tool use was denied"), and the run went on without it. A write-in-worktree agent could no longer edit files.
- Handing the token over a file descriptor instead, as the CLI's `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN_FILE_DESCRIPTOR` allows (a pipe at descriptor 3, the variable naming it; the CLI reads the pipe at start-up and drops the variable): the fake API got the token, no exec block held it (0 of about 700, confined), descriptor 3 gave the tools nothing, and the `Write` call succeeded, confined and not.
- Linux, unchecked on 2026-09-30: Docker was not running, no Linux host with a login was at hand, and CI has no `claude`. Checked on 2026-10-08 by OWL-101, in a Linux container, not on a Linux host: `OWLSHIFT_LIVE_CLAUDE=1 cargo test -p owlshift-runner --test agent_live -- --ignored` passed (`test result: ok. 1 passed`, 2.70 s). It ran a real confined `claude -p` on the maintainer's `claude setup-token` token, and the run completed on the subscription.
  - **Where.** Docker Desktop 4.70.0 on the maintainer's Mac, linux/aarch64, kernel 6.12.76-linuxkit. The image was Debian bookworm with bubblewrap 0.8.0 and Claude Code 2.1.294 from npm, built from `main` at `e0b59e4`. The run user was not root.
  - **How the token got there.** The maintainer ran the script in their own terminal. It piped the token from the macOS Keychain into the container's standard input, so the token was never in an argument, a variable or a file on the Mac. There, a probe stored it in gnome-keyring through the Secret Service (keyring on a tmpfs, unlocked with a throwaway password in a `dbus-launch` session). The probe called the same `Keychain::system().store` as `owlshift init`, with the same service and account; `owlshift init` itself did not run. The test then read the token back as the executor does.
  - **A dry run first.** The same rig with a made-up token ended on API status 401, "OAuth access token is invalid". So under bwrap the CLI read the token from the descriptor it was given.
  - **What the container changes.** It ran with `--security-opt seccomp=unconfined --security-opt systempaths=unconfined`, which bwrap needs inside Docker; a host has neither. The Docker Desktop kernel has no AppArmor restriction on unprivileged user namespaces, so the Ubuntu 24.04 path (the `bwrap` profile, CI above) was not exercised.
  - **What this settles.** A confined Linux run reads its login from the descriptor, starts and completes on the subscription.
  - **What it does not.** The container's fresh home holds no credential, so the test's credential and MCP checks found nothing to find: home isolation rests on the CI confinement tests above. The `ptrace` residual risk (OWL-94, above) is not touched, and a bare-metal host or an x86_64 machine was not checked.

Decision (2026-09-30, OWL-96): the harness command does not set `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB`. It does not keep the token from the agent, which reads it from the `claude` process's exec block, and it would deny every file edit of a write-in-worktree run. The agent environment inherits no `CLAUDE_*` variable from the runner (`INHERITED`), so an operator's own setting does not reach the harness.

Decision (2026-09-30, OWL-96, extended by the maintainer to the fix): the executor hands the token over a file descriptor, and never sets `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`.

- **How.** `HarnessLogin::apply` calls `process::hand_over_on_descriptor`: it writes the token and a newline into a pipe before the spawn, closes the write end, moves the read end to a descriptor above 2, close-on-exec in the runner, and makes it inheritable in the child alone (a `pre_exec` hook that makes one `fcntl` call, the only `unsafe` of `owlshift-platform` on Unix). The variable `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN_FILE_DESCRIPTOR` names it. The runner's copy closes when the command is dropped, right after the spawn. A token holding a newline, or longer than 511 bytes, is refused before anything runs; the keychain's one-word check refuses the first already, and a `claude setup-token` token is about 110 bytes. The one write of 512 bytes at most is atomic and never waits for a reader. Native Windows runs no agent (D9), and there the call fails.
- **No fallback.** An older CLI that does not know the variable finds no login, and the run fails; the token never goes back into an environment.
- **Rejected.** `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` (above). A fixed descriptor 3 through `dup2`: more `unsafe`, and the CLI reads any number. A temporary file: the token would sit on disk.
- **Tests.** Unit tests of `hand_over_on_descriptor`: the child reads exactly the token, once, and its environment does not hold it. A process the runner starts while the handed-over command still lives does not have the descriptor. A token with a newline, or too long, is refused without being shown. The confinement bench runs through `Executor::run`, under Seatbelt on macOS and under bwrap on the Linux CI job. Its harness reads the token from the descriptor, and its probe checks the rest without showing the token: `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` unset; the raw environment not holding the token; a process started after the read getting nothing from the descriptor; and, where there is a `/proc`, exactly one process inside the sandbox whose exec block holds the token, the control it started with the token on purpose. Passed on the maintainer's macOS machine on 2026-09-30, and on the Linux CI job the same day (ubuntu-latest with the repository's bwrap AppArmor profile, `OWLSHIFT_REQUIRE_CONFINEMENT=1`, so not skipped): bwrap passes the descriptor through, and inside its sandbox only the control's exec block held the token.
- **A real `claude` login through the descriptor.**
  - On macOS, checked on 2026-10-09 by OWL-206 on the maintainer's machine (macOS 26.6.2, Claude Code 2.1.284), from `main` at `a68505b`: `OWLSHIFT_LIVE_CLAUDE=1 cargo test -p owlshift-runner --test agent_live -- --ignored` passed (`test result: ok. 1 passed`, 8.63 s). The maintainer ran it in their own terminal. It read the token for agent runs from the macOS keychain and handed it over on the descriptor to a real `claude -p`, confined as the executor confines it (Seatbelt on macOS). The run completed on the subscription, and the credential probe and the run's MCP check found nothing.
  - On Linux, the same command passed on 2026-10-08 under bwrap in a Linux container on macOS, not on a Linux host. OWL-101 has the details and limits (above).

**OWL-100 — the operator's own `/tmp/claude-<uid>`: live check.** During the OWL-96 checks a confined `claude -p` exited 1 at start-up with "Error processing settings: EPERM: operation not permitted, open '/tmp/claude-501'". Checked on 2026-09-30 on the maintainer's machine (macOS 26.6.2, Claude Code 2.1.284), with `/private/tmp/claude-501` present (0700, made by the user's own interactive sessions, as on any Mac where the operator also uses Claude Code). The command was built as `agent_live` builds it: `AgentEnv::from_runner`, `ClaudeHarness::sandbox_needs`, the adapter's read-only argv, `AgentEnv::confine` (the real Seatbelt profile), `HarnessLogin::apply` with a made-up token and an empty `harness-config`. With a made-up token a run that starts ends on API status 401, so a 401 means start-up passed.

- Without `CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR`: the run exits before any request, with the error above. Reproduced. The CLI keeps its temporary files in `claude-<uid>` under that variable, else under `/tmp`, and the sandbox closes `/private/tmp`. The same folder is what stopped the Bash tool in OWL-65 (above).
- With `CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR` naming a folder in the run's own temporary folder: status 401, so start-up passed, and the CLI made `claude-501` in that folder. It did so as well when the folder did not exist: the CLI makes it, so the runner makes and binds nothing more. The run's temporary folder is already written in both sandboxes.
- The peer inbox (OWL-65): with the variable set and `CLAUDE_CODE_HARBOR_KITE` removed from the command, the CLI also made `cc-socks`, where it binds the socket, in that folder. With `CLAUDE_CODE_HARBOR_KITE=0`, as `from_runner` sets it, it made none. The guard holds, and from now on it is what keeps the socket unbound.
- Not checked: Linux. Under bwrap `/tmp` is private and empty, so the user's folder is not there and the run was not broken; the variable is set on every platform all the same, one behaviour for both.

Decision (2026-09-30, OWL-100): a confined Claude Code run keeps its temporary files in `harness-tmp`, inside the run's own temporary folder.

- **How.** The harness names the variable in `SandboxNeeds::temp_variable`: `ClaudeHarness` names `CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR` (`claude::TMPDIR_ENV`) when the agent is confined, and a bare run of the test bench is unchanged. The executor sets it on the harness command alone to `<run temporary folder>/harness-tmp` (`executor::HARNESS_TEMP_DIR`), after the command is confined; a gate command does not get it. The CLI makes the folder, and it is removed with the run. Nothing is opened in the sandbox: no bind, no shared temporary folder.
- **Rejected.** A third field of `HarnessLogin`: a temporary folder is not a login. The run's temporary folder itself: it works, but a folder of its own keeps the CLI's per-project folders apart from the agent's own `TMPDIR` files. Making and binding the folder like `harness-config`: not needed, since the CLI makes it.
- **Residual risk.** The variable is not documented: a release that drops it fails every confined run at start-up on macOS, loudly. With the inbox reopened by this variable, a release that drops `CLAUDE_CODE_HARBOR_KITE` too leaves the refuse flag alone to refuse messages (OWL-65).
- **Tests.** The unit test of `ClaudeHarness::sandbox_needs` checks that a confined run names the variable and a bare one does not. The bench's `a_confined_harness_keeps_its_temporary_files_clear_of_the_operators_folder`, in `tests/confinement.rs`, runs through `Executor::run` a shell-script harness that keeps its files as the CLI does, in `claude-<uid>` under `CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR`, and fails if the variable is unset. It writes there, the folder lies in the run's `TMPDIR`, and it cannot list the user's `/tmp/claude-<uid>`: denied on macOS, where the test makes the folder if it is absent, and absent under bwrap on Linux. Passed on the maintainer's macOS machine on 2026-09-30. `agent_live` sets the variable as the executor does. `peer_inbox.rs` still checks that the confined command carries `CLAUDE_CODE_HARBOR_KITE=0`.

**OWL-71 — launching agent commands on native Windows (decided 2026-09-29).** Native confinement (OWL-58) was split: this part prepared the launch path, and OWL-72 was to apply an AppContainer through it. Decision D9 is unchanged: native Windows runs no agent. Superseded (2026-09-29): native confinement is set aside, and the launcher was removed (OWL-89, 2026-09-30); see the decision after the OWL-72 results below. What follows is the record of what was built and checked; none of it is in the code any more.

- Premise, checked on 2026-09-29: `std::process::Command` on stable Rust cannot pass a proc-thread attribute, which is how an AppContainer is given (`SECURITY_CAPABILITIES`). A scratch crate calling `ProcThreadAttributeList::build().raw_attribute(..)` and `CommandExt::spawn_with_attributes`, checked with `cargo check --target x86_64-pc-windows-msvc` on rustc 1.98.1, fails with E0658, "use of unstable library feature `windows_process_extensions_raw_attribute`" (issue #114854).
- The fork: a `CreateProcessW` path of the runner's own, with a child type of its own, or a small program the runner starts as it starts any command, which calls `CreateProcessW` itself. Decision: the program, `owlshift-launch`. A child type of our own would replace `std::process::Child` wherever the runner takes one (the `Harness::drive` trait, the Claude Code adapter's `drive`, `drive_plain`, `watch::spawn`, the gate, `process::run_command` for the probes), and redo in Windows-only unsafe code what std does: pipes, the environment block, the program's lookup, argument quoting. The launcher keeps `sandbox::wrap -> Command`, as `sandbox-exec` and `bwrap` do; the cost is one more process per agent command and a second binary.
- As built, until its removal in OWL-89 (2026-09-30):
  - `owlshift-launch` is a second binary of `owlshift-cli`, whose `default-run = "owlshift"` keeps `cargo owl` and `bacon owl` working. Its code is `owlshift_platform::launch`, on Windows only; elsewhere the binary refuses to run.
  - Its arguments are `-- PROGRAM LINE`, `LINE` being the rest of the program's command line, verbatim. On Windows `sandbox::wrap` quotes the arguments into `LINE` as std quotes a regular argument, and `sandbox::wrap_line` takes a line as it is, for `cmd.exe`. The gate goes through `AgentEnv::confine_shell`: `confine` rebuilds a command from `get_args`, which hands back the gate's `raw_arg` as a plain argument, to be quoted again.
  - The runner starts the launcher as the root of the run's process tree, inside its Job Object (`ProcessTree::spawn`, unchanged). The launcher finds the program as std does, but for the runner's own folder and `PATH`: `.exe` is added when there is no extension, and a name with no folder is looked for in the absolute folders of the agent's `PATH`, then `SYSTEMROOT\System32` and `SYSTEMROOT`, never in the working directory. It creates the program with `CreateProcessW` and its three standard handles, closes its own copies, waits, and exits with the program's exit code; its own failures exit with 127 and `owlshift-launch: <reason>` on standard error. A job's process starts its children in the job, since `tree.rs` allows no breakaway, so stopping the tree stops the launcher, the program and all it started.
  - Batch files are refused, by `wrap_line` and again by the launcher on the path it found: `.bat` and `.cmd` in any letter case, trailing dots and spaces ignored, and a name holding `:`, a file stream such as `x.bat::$DATA`. `cmd.exe` reads a batch file's arguments again; std guards that case with its own escaping, the launcher does not. So the npm-installed Claude Code, `claude.cmd`, cannot be launched, and the native installer's `claude.exe` can.
- Nothing is granted or denied yet. A shipped build has no launcher: on Windows `wrap` still refuses with `Unsupported`, as `available` does, and `Executor::run` and `owlshift do` refuse before anything is written. Only a test sets one, with `sandbox::use_built_launcher`, compiled with the platform crate's `testkit` feature, which the runner's tests enable: a thread-local override naming the binary Cargo built, so a refusal test running beside it still sees none. `the_cli_never_turns_confinement_off` refuses the name in the CLI's sources, since a workspace build unifies the feature into `owlshift`. The work that would have lifted the refusal, finding the launcher next to the running `owlshift`, confining the program it creates and lifting `available`, is not planned; OWL-72, below, recorded how the candidate confinements behave before it was set aside.
- Checked on windows-latest on 2026-09-29 ([run 36591257645](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/36591257645)):
  - `process::tests::a_launched_program_is_stopped_with_its_launcher_at_the_deadline`: the helper's parent was `owlshift-launch.exe`, and at a 4 s deadline the launcher, the helper and its grandchild were all gone.
  - `executor::harness::tests::a_confined_harness_command_starts_through_the_launcher`, on the harness's path, `AgentEnv::confine`: an empty argument, `a b`, `a"b`, `a\"b`, `c:\x\` and a tab came back unchanged, as did the standard input, the agent's placeholder `GH_TOKEN` and exit code 3.
  - `executor::gate`'s Windows tests, bare and confined: `echo "a  b" \"q\" "c:\x\" && exit 5` logged its line as written and failed with exit status 5, the agent environment was set, and a command past a 1 s deadline was stopped.
  - The refusals held: `native_windows_refuses_to_confine`, `a_confined_gate_command_is_refused_on_native_windows` and `a_run_on_native_windows_is_refused`.
- Limits: the Windows launcher tests need the launcher built, which `cargo test --workspace` does and a test run of one package alone does not (the test names `cargo build -p owlshift-cli --bin owlshift-launch`); `cargo owl` builds `owlshift` only, so a Windows run, once native confinement lands, will need that build too; an argument full of quotes reaches the 32,767-character limit of a command line at about half its length, since `LINE` is quoted once more on the launcher's own command line; Ctrl-C through the launcher was not checked live.

**OWL-72 — how an AppContainer behaves on windows-latest (checked 2026-09-29).** OWL-72 was re-scoped to live checks before any confinement code; two follow-ups (OWL-83, OWL-84) were to rest on them, and were canceled when native Windows was set aside (decision below). Decision D9 stands: native Windows runs no agent. A throwaway program calling the Win32 API through `windows-sys` ran in a temporary workflow on the OWL-72 branch, removed before the pull request, on windows-latest (Windows Server 2025, 10.0.26100.33438), in four rounds: [run 36603469888](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/36603469888), [36604300313](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/36604300313), [36604775652](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/36604775652) and [36605235445](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/36605235445); its source is in the pull request's commits. The secrets were fake: a gh-shaped `hosts.yml` and a sibling project's `.env` planted in the user's temporary folder, and a generic Credential Manager entry the program wrote and deleted. The container was a profile made with `CreateAppContainerProfile`, given to `CreateProcessW` as `SECURITY_CAPABILITIES` through `STARTUPINFOEXW`, as the launcher would give it.

- **Files are closed by default.** Granted nothing, the container's process read `System32` but neither the planted token nor the `.env` ("Access is denied", exit 1), itself or through a grandchild. It could not list the profile or `%APPDATA%`, nor read a file in the runner's workspace on `D:`, outside the profile. It reaches only what grants ALL APPLICATION PACKAGES (`System32`, `Program Files`) or its own SID.
