- **Follow-ups.** A gate-specific attempt counter, if one fix run proves too few: decided against on 2026-10-10 (OWL-208, answering OWL-55). The one red gate of the P1 and P2 deliveries (OWL-174's timing flake, OWL-177) passed at its single retry, so the gate keeps `MAX_FAILED_RUNS`; what P3 bounds instead is the fix passes after delivery ("Watch after delivery", above). The isolation check's own `git status` still runs with the runner's environment, now only once the link and the shared git files are found untouched (OWL-59). The gate's own checks around its commands run git in the worktree with the agent environment, no hook and no monitor, outside the sandbox; OWL-64 makes the executor's file-system checks (the link, the shared git files) before that git, before the commands and again after them, and a violation stops the gate there and quarantines the run, so a clean filter the gate's code configured never runs; the gate's status ignores submodules, so a planted gitlink starts no git in an unchecked repository. One limit remains: a process that escaped the gate's tree could change those files after the check.
  - *Configured filter drivers* (OWL-73, decided 2026-09-29). The worktree's attributes, written by the run, choose the filter driver a file goes through, and a driver already configured (a global or system git-lfs, one in the repository's own configuration, such as a script in the worktree) would run in the gate's status outside the sandbox, on content the run chose. Just before each of the gate's two status calls, the gate lists every driver git would find (`git config -z --get-regexp '^filter\.'`, with the same environment and hardening as the status) and switches each off on that status: `-c filter.<name>.clean=`, `smudge=`, `process=` and `required=false`. This departs from the ticket's two options. Running the gate's git without the global and system configuration leaves the repository's own drivers running and loses the global `core.excludesFile`, so the gate would fail on files the user ignores. Recording the drivers in the snapshot and failing closed when attributes name one would fail every gate of a repository that uses git-lfs, whose committed attributes name it; `merge.*.driver` never runs in a status. Two costs: a file a driver filters is read raw, so once its timestamps change (touched, or racily clean after a checkout or a commit) it reads as modified and the gate fails, which a repository using git-lfs should expect, the failure naming the drivers switched off; and a driver whose name a `-c` argument cannot carry (holding `=`, empty, not UTF-8) fails every gate, as does a listing git could not give. Checked on 2026-09-29 with git 2.54.0, a throwaway global configuration and a marker per driver: the gate's status and `rev-parse` ran no `diff.*.textconv`, `diff.*.command` or `merge.*.driver`, while `git diff` ran the diff command; the empty values stopped a clean driver and a `process` driver, whereas keeping `required=true` made the status die ("clean filter 'X' failed"); `-c filter.a=b.clean=` split at the first `=` and the `a=b` driver still ran; the listing gave every scope's keys, a name's case kept. A possible follow-up covers every program git could start at once: running the gate's own git inside the sandbox, where a driver still runs, but confined.

**A refused result** (OWL-180, decided 2026-10-07). On the P2 exit gate (OWL-175, below), a Build run asking after a round with a re-ask went on from the thread's numbering and asked `Q4`; the runner refused its `result.json` ("question Q4 is out of order: expected Q1"), its retry, told nothing, did the same, and the second failed run parked the ticket.

- **The role.** `roles/build.md` says that every run that asks numbers its questions from `Q1`, whatever ids the thread's earlier rounds and re-asks used.
- **The retry is told why.** A Build run whose `result.json` is refused (`Failure::InvalidResult`) is a failed run, as before, and the next Build run of the same command gets the reason in its brief (`result_refusal`, brief format 7; `result.json` stays format 5), as a red gate's next run gets `gate_failure`. `on_demand::result_refusal` reads it from the run's outcome for `OnDemand::build` and for the scenarios' stand-in driver alike: any other outcome leaves the next brief without it, so it reaches one run only, and never the answer check or the resolver (OWL-184, below, tells the answer check its own). The reason is the runner's message, cut to 2 KiB (`MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES`) with its beginning kept, since it can quote a whole string of the refused result; what it quotes is data. A result holding the harness's login is a credential failure, checked before the contract (`executor::read_result`), so the reason never quotes it.
- **What it did not change.** The failed-run breaker: a refused run counted, so after another failed run at the same stage the refusal parked the ticket with no retry left to tell; OWL-183, below, keeps it from spending that last attempt. The reason lived in the command's memory, like `gate_failure`: a park, a usage limit or the end of the command dropped it, until OWL-192 kept it in the ticket ref ("A Build refusal kept across commands" below). The answer check is retried across commands, so carrying its refusals needs the ticket ref: OWL-184, below, does it, and records why the resolver gets none.
- **Rejected: the runner renumbering the questions itself.** The numbering carries nothing but order, but renumbering would loosen a strict contract for one shape only, while the brief's reason serves every refusal a run can fix by itself (an unknown field, `pr` without `done`, a wrong `pr.branch`). And a run's own text can cite its ids, in a question's `context` or a decision's `basis`, which a silent renumbering would leave pointing at another question.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/numbering.toml` is the acceptance: after a round with a re-ask, a run asking `Q3` is refused with nothing posted, and its retry, told why, opens round 2 with `Q1`. `a_refused_result_is_told_to_the_next_build_run_only` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the real loop: the refusal reaches the next Build brief only, not the resolver's nor the Build run after, and `run_started` records it.

**A refused result at the last attempt** (OWL-183, decided 2026-10-07). With OWL-180 alone, a refused Build result was one more failed run: when another run of the stage had already failed, it was the second and parked the ticket, and its reason reached no run. A failed answer check counts toward the stage it returns to, so a round whose check failed once, followed by a resumed Build run that numbers on from the thread as OWL-175's did, parked with that fix unused.

- **The rule.** A Build run whose `result.json` is refused, and whose brief carried no `result_refusal`, counts as a failed run, except that it never brings the count to the breaker's limit: at the stage's last attempt, the ticket stays at Build with its count, nothing is posted, and the next Build run of the command gets that attempt, told why. A Build run that was told and fails, however, counts as any failed run, so at the last attempt it parks. Below the last attempt nothing changes: the first refusal of a ticket with no failed run counts, and its told retry is the last attempt.
- **Where.** The core event `Event::ResultRefused`, valid at Build, since only Build's brief carried a refusal's reason, and since OWL-190 while waiting for input too, where the answer check runs ("A refused answer check at the last attempt" below): it counts as `RunFailed` below the last attempt and leaves the state as it is at it, so `state.json` keeps its format and its invariants. The runner chooses it: `on_demand::stage_event` maps a Build run whose outcome `result_refusal` tells to it when the run's brief carried no `result_refusal` (`on_demand::freed_refusal`), and every other outcome as `core_event` does, for `OnDemand::build` and the stand-in driver alike. It covers every refusal `result_refusal` tells, the contract's and a result that is a link or past 1 MiB alike, and since OWL-184 an artifact the run named wrongly.
- **The bound.** Two refusals in a row never both go free: the second run was told. Each freed refusal is followed by a told run that parks, stops (a round for the decider, a usage limit), delivers, or goes on through a resolver's settled pass or a late-comment integration run; neither count is reset in a command (`MAX_RESOLVED_PASSES`, one integration per delivery), so a command frees a handful of refusals at most, each at the cost of one Build run. Across commands Build's reason was dropped, as OWL-180 said, so a `continue` after a usage limit could free a refusal again; since OWL-192 the ticket ref keeps it, the Build run of a later command is told, and its refusal counts, so the bound holds across commands too. The PARKED comment says "a run failed again, with no attempt left", true whether or not a freed refusal came before. The answer check's freed refusals have a bound of their own (OWL-190, below).
- **Rejected.** Counting as before and documenting the park: OWL-180's fix would never reach the case it was built for. A per-stage flag in `state.json` (format 3), set once a refusal was freed: the reason it pairs with lived in the command's memory, so the flag would outlive what it is for (OWL-192 keeps the reason in `questions.json`, and the told brief is the flag). A refusal that never counts: two in a row, and then more, would loop on paid runs. The rule in the runner alone, skipping the core event: the breakers live in the core machine, and the stand-in driver would need a copy. Freeing the contract's refusals only, not a link or a size: it would split `Failure::InvalidResult` for no case seen. The same rule frees a refused artifact once OWL-184 tells it: what is told is what is freed.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/last_attempt.toml` is the acceptance: a crashed answer check counts at Build, the resumed Build run asks `Q2` and is refused with nothing posted or parked, and its retry, told why, opens round 2 with `Q1`. `a_refused_result_never_spends_the_last_attempt` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the real loop and the bound: after a run with no result, a refused run does not park, and its told retry, refused again, does. `a_refused_result_counts_but_never_spends_the_last_attempt` (`owlshift-core`) and the transition table pin the event.

**A refused answer check** (OWL-184, decided 2026-10-07). OWL-180 told only the next Build run of a command why its predecessor's `result.json` was refused. The answer check is retried too, by a later `continue` or watch, and a refused artifact (a file a result names in `artifacts`) was told to no run; either could repeat the same fixable mistake.

- **The answer check is told.** A check whose `result.json` is refused is a failed run, as before, and the next check on the same ask gets the reason in its brief's `result_refusal` (brief format 8, which allows the field for `build` and `answer_check`, and still refuses it for the resolver). Its retry is always a later command, so the reason is kept in the ticket ref, on the latest ask of `questions.json` (`Ask::result_refusal`, format 5), where the latest check's verdicts already live: a re-ask starts a new ask, so a reason never outlives the ask its check judged. `answer_check::keep`, for `OnDemand::check_answers` and the stand-in driver alike, keeps it with the verdicts, before the state moves, so a park keeps it too: a refused result sets it, any other kept outcome clears it (verdicts, a failed or crashed check, a quarantine), and a usage limit keeps nothing, so the next check is told what the interrupted one was. `TicketQuestions::validate` refuses a kept reason that is blank or past `MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES`.
- **At the last attempt.** A refused check first counted as any failed check, so after another failed run at the stage it parked; the reason stayed in the ref through the park, and the person's `continue` that restarted the ticket ran a told check. OWL-190 frees it as OWL-183 frees Build's ("A refused answer check at the last attempt" below).
- **The resolver was not told.** A refused resolver result is never retried as such: `resolver::outcome` makes it a fallback, and every question goes to the decider in a round that says why; a refusal costs the decider a question, never a loop. One case replays the same questions: a fallback round whose post or keeping failed, after which a later command's Build run may ask them again and the resolver run again. OWL-191 tells that run ("A refused resolver result told to its replay" below).
- **Refused artifacts are told.** `executor::Failure::refusal` says which failures are the run's own to fix: a refused `result.json`, and an artifact refused for its path or its file (`ArtifactError::is_the_runs`: a path that is not plain, a link, a second name, not a regular file, missing, too large). An artifact deleted while the runner read it, a worktree the runner cannot use, or a failing file system is not the run's mistake and is not told. The reason is the runner's message, such as "artifact `plan`: `.owlshift/run/plan.md` does not exist". It reaches Build's next run in the command and the answer check's next check alike, and OWL-183's freeing covers it since `stage_event` frees what `result_refusal` tells, as OWL-190's covers the answer check's.
- **Rejected.** The reason in `state.json`: the core state keeps no text, and OWL-183 already turned down a per-stage flag there. A top-level field of `questions.json`: every new ask would have to clear it. A third file in the ticket ref's tree: a format of its own for one string.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/check_refusal.toml` is the acceptance: round 1 asks Q1, a check giving its verdict on Q2 is refused with nothing posted (a counted `result_refused` since OWL-190, no run having failed before it), the next check stops at a usage limit, told why, and the one after it, told the same, answers the round. `a_refused_check_is_told_to_the_next_check_across_commands` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the ticket ref across commands and a park: after a crashed check, a refused one keeps its reason without parking (OWL-190), its told retry, refused again, parks keeping the new reason, and the restart's check is told and clears it. `a_refused_artifact_is_told_and_never_spends_the_last_attempt` checks a Build result naming a missing plan after a failed run. The contracts tests pin the brief's role check, the format-4 read and the kept reason's bounds; `on_demand`'s unit test which artifact refusals are told.

**A refused answer check at the last attempt** (OWL-190, decided 2026-10-08). With OWL-184 alone, a refused answer-check result was one more failed run: after a crashed check, the refusal parked the ticket, posted a PARKED comment, and took it out of watch's reach until a person typed `continue`, though the runner already knew what to tell the next check.

- **The rule.** OWL-183's, for the answer check: a check whose `result.json` (or an artifact it names) is refused, and whose brief carried no `result_refusal`, counts as a failed run, except that it never brings the count to the breaker's limit. At the last attempt the ticket keeps waiting for input with its count, nothing is posted, the latest ask keeps the reason (OWL-184), and the command stops as for any failed check (`Stop::CheckFailed`). A check that was told and fails counts as any failed run, so at the last attempt it parks. Below the last attempt nothing changes.
- **Where.** `Event::ResultRefused`, valid while waiting for input as at Build, so the breakers stay in the core machine and `state.json` keeps its format. `answer_check::event` takes the check's brief and maps a refusal to it when `on_demand::freed_refusal` says so, the test `stage_event` makes for Build, for `OnDemand::check_answers` and the stand-in driver alike; `answer_check::keep` keeps the reason for it as for a counted refusal.
- **When the retry runs.** The next check, as for any failed check: its retry is always a later command (OWL-184). Watch runs it at its next pass, since the kept reason moves the ticket ref and the 10-minute hold applies only to a continue that left the ref as it was; a person typing `continue` runs it too. Each such pass costs one check, until the told one gives its verdicts, fails or parks.
- **The bound.** Two refusals in a row never both go free: the reason is kept in the ticket ref, so the next check is told whatever command runs it, through a usage limit or a restart, and its refusal counts. A check that gives its verdicts clears the reason; a counter-question, which moves no counter, or a re-ask, which starts a new ask, can therefore be followed by another freed refusal. Each such cycle needs a new comment of the decider before a check runs again, so the runner never loops alone; the re-ask limit bounds the re-asks, and nothing bounds counter-questions but the decider, as before.
- **Rejected.** Retrying in the same command, `check_answers` running a told check right away: a second paid run in one command and a loop in the check's path and the stand-in driver, for a retry watch already makes at its next pass. Leaving it as OWL-184 left it: the park cost a PARKED comment on the ticket and a person's gesture for a mistake the runner can tell.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/check_last_attempt.toml` is the acceptance: round 1 asks Q1, the first check crashes, the second gives its verdict on Q2 and is refused with nothing posted or parked, and the third, told why, answers the round. `watch_retries_a_refused_check_at_the_last_attempt` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) plays it under watch, the told retry at the next pass, with no PARKED comment and no `continue` typed; `a_refused_check_is_told_to_the_next_check_across_commands` checks the bound across commands. `a_refused_check_is_freed_unless_it_was_told` (`answer_check`), `a_refused_result_counts_but_never_spends_the_last_attempt` (`owlshift-core`) and the transition table pin the event.

**A retry after refused decisions asks** (OWL-186, decided 2026-10-07). OWL-176 refused a Build result that lists `decisions` and told the next Build run of the command why, but nothing checked that run: one that dropped the refused decisions and reported `done` delivered the choices unasked, the silent settling OWL-176 set out to stop.

- **The rule.** The Build run told that its predecessor's decisions were refused may not end `done`: `RunResult::validate_against` refuses its `done`, whatever else it lists, and lets every other status through (`questions` asks; `blocked`, `premise_false` and `failed` stop without settling anything). The rule bounds `done` only: it does not check that each refused choice is asked, which OWL-193 does ("A retry asks every refused choice" below).
- **The kind.** The runner knows the refusal was of decisions from a typed flag, not from the reason's text. `RunResult::parse_against` checks the build role's decisions right after the document's shape, before every other rule, and says so (`result::Refused::decisions`, since OWL-195 the kind `result::DecisionsRefusal`: `Listed` or `Done`), so a result that lists decisions is refused for them whatever else it breaks; the executor makes that refusal `Failure::Decisions`, which carries the kind, told like `Failure::InvalidResult` and so freed at the last attempt by OWL-183; `on_demand::decisions_kind` reads it, for `OnDemand::build` and the stand-in driver alike, and the next Build brief of the command carries `decisions_refused` (brief format 9: in Build's brief only, beside its `result_refusal`; `result.json` stays format 6). The refusal of a `done` under the flag is a decisions refusal too, since the choices are still open; while `MAX_FAILED_RUNS` is 2 that reaches no run, the told run's refusal parking the ticket.
- **The retry knows what to ask.** The refused result is removed before the next run, so the decisions reason now quotes the choices it listed, each question with what was recorded, after the advice and within `MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES` (a long list is cut, its beginning kept). A told run that lists decisions again gets a short advice before its choices (OWL-195): its list is told after the first one, whose full advice comes first ("A Build refusal kept across commands" below).
- **The role.** `roles/build.md`: with `decisions_refused`, ask each refused choice, even one found settled since by the decider's word, a `decision` entry, a rule or the repository, its `context` naming what settles it (the one exception to never asking again what the decider's word settles), and end with any status but `done`.
- **False refusals.** A retry that finds a refused choice settled since (the decider commented between the two runs, or the refused run listed as a decision what only followed the thread, a rule or the repository) still may not end `done`: it asks. Since OWL-222 such a question goes to the decider, never to the resolver, so it costs the decider a question, its `context` naming what settles it. That is a question, never a wrong decision, and the refused run had ignored the prompt first. A retry that ends `done` anyway is a told run's refusal, a failed run that parks the ticket (OWL-183's bound), and its PARKED comment quotes the reason to the person who restarts it.
- **The live run.** The ticket asked to weigh the rule against OWL-176's live acceptance run first; none is recorded (OWL-176's acceptance is `open_points.toml`, and the live runs this document records, the P2 exit gate's, came before it), and none could be made when this was decided. The rule does not rest on one: a retry that asks as told never meets it, so a live run showing retries that ask would make it costless, not unneeded, and OWL-176 rejected the prompt alone because nothing outside the run checks it.
- **What remains.** A told retry that asks some refused choices and drops others went unseen, the rule reading the status, until OWL-193 (below). The flag lived in the command's memory with the reason (OWL-180), so a park, a usage limit or the end of the command dropped it and the first Build run of a later command was untold; OWL-192 keeps both in the ticket ref ("A Build refusal kept across commands" below). A result whose shape is broken (an unknown field, a wrong type) is refused for it before its decisions are read. Choices never recorded stay unseen (OWL-176).
- **Rejected.** No rule, the design recording why: OWL-176's reason for refusing the prompt alone holds. Reading the kind from the reason's text: the reason is the runner's free message, reworded at will and able to quote the refused result, where a typed flag is checked by `Brief::validate`. Letting a `done` through when the thread changed since the refused run: a comment that settles nothing would unlock it, and the runner cannot tell which comment settles a choice. Requiring as many questions as refused decisions: a run may merge or split choices, or find one followed, so the count would refuse good retries and pass bad ones. The runner asking the refused decisions itself: a decision carries neither the category the routing needs nor a question's context, and the decider would get the run's wording of a choice already built.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/open_points_unasked.toml` is the acceptance, on `open_points`' fixture: the first Build run decides both points and is refused; its retry, told why, settles one in a commit the gate would pass and reports `done`; that result is refused before the gate, nothing is pushed, and the ticket parks with a PARKED comment quoting the reason (without the rule, the retry is delivered). `open_points.toml` keeps the retry that asks. `a_refused_result_is_told_to_the_next_build_run_only` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the real loop: a refusal is told to the next Build brief only, not the resolver's nor the Build run after, and a decisions refusal flags that brief, whose question then goes to the decider (OWL-222). `decisions_against_the_brief` (`owlshift-contracts`) pins the rule under every status, the quoted choices, and decisions refused before a misnumbered question; the executor's unit test and `on_demand`'s pin the failure's kind and that it is told.

**A Build refusal kept across commands** (OWL-192, decided 2026-10-08). OWL-180's reason and OWL-186's flag lived in the command's memory: after a refusal of decisions, a park, a usage limit or the end of the command dropped them, and the first Build run of a later command (`continue`, watch's continue, `do`) was told neither why nor that it may not end `done`, so the refused choices could be settled silently. The hold now outlives the command.

- **Where.** In the ticket ref, as a top-level field of `questions.json` (`TicketQuestions::build_refusal`, format 6): since format 8 (OWL-195, decided 2026-10-08), the reason of every refusal that listed choices since no Build run asked, oldest first (`lists`, which hold the next runs while not empty), and the latest refusal's reason when it listed none (`reason`, absent when the latest is the last list). Format 6 and 7 kept the hold as a flag (`decisions`), the latest reason and, once a later refusal took its place, the hold's first list (`decisions_reason`); each is read through its own shape and upgraded: the held reason becomes the first list and the latest reason, of a kind those formats did not keep, a list after it, so no later refusal drops it, told where it was; a hold without a held reason becomes one list. Since format 9 (OWL-198, decided 2026-10-08), a refusal that lists choices keeps them typed (`choices`, `result::RefusedChoice`: each decision's question and what it recorded, its basis left out, each text cut to `MAX_CHOICE_BYTES`, 512 bytes, its beginning kept), carried from `RunResult::parse_against` in the refusal's kind (`DecisionsRefusal::Listed`), at most `MAX_REFUSED_CHOICES`, 64, those past it counted (`left_out`); `lists` holds only what formats 6 to 8 kept as text, and a format-8 document is read with its lists as they were. OWL-184 kept the answer check's on its ask, and turned down a top-level field because every new ask would have to clear it; a Build refusal belongs to no ask, and a new ask must not clear it, so the top level is its place. `state.json` keeps no text (OWL-183, OWL-184). `TicketQuestions::validate` refuses the reason or a list blank or past `MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES`, more than 64 choices or a choice's text past 512 bytes, choices counted as left out while there is room for them, and a refusal that keeps neither a reason nor a choice.
- **When.** `on_demand::build_refusal_after`, for `OnDemand::build` and the stand-in driver alike, after each Build run that did not break isolation: a refusal `result_refusal` tells takes the kept reason's place, except that one that lists choices, as the failure's kind says (`DecisionsRefusal::Listed`, never the reason's text), is added to the lists (since OWL-198, its choices to the kept ones, `BuildRefusal::keep_choices`), and every later refusal keeps them, so a later refusal for another fault (a broken shape, a refused `done`) or another list never drops one; a reason that lists none is kept only while it is the latest, holding no choice; an accepted result (a valid `result.json`, or a `done` whose gate failed) clears a refusal of anything but decisions; a hold is cleared only by a result that asks, `questions` or `premise_false` (since OWL-193, one with a question), since a `blocked` or `failed` result asked nothing and a `done` is refused under it; a usage limit, a run that failed with nothing told and a quarantine leave it, so the next run is told the same. A told run that crashed no longer drops the flag for the next run of the same command either.
- **What the run is told.** Every Build brief, whatever the command, carries the kept reason in `result_refusal` and the hold in `decisions_refused` (`on_demand::tell_build`, `BuildRefusal::told`): every list, oldest first, then the latest reason when it listed none, each after "A later result was refused too:", within `MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES` (OWL-195). OWL-194 told the hold's first list and the latest reason only, so from the third refusal a list between them was not told, and cut the whole text with its beginning kept, so a long first list cut the later ones off. Items that do not fit whole now share the room: one shorter than its share stays whole, the others are cut to it, each keeping its beginning and marked `[cut]`. A refusal's choices come after its advice, so the share must leave room for them: a told run that lists decisions again gets a short advice (`RunResult::parse_against` reads the brief's `decisions_refused`), its list always following the first one, whose full advice is told first; three long lists and a refused `done` then each tell the start of their choices. Past ten items, the most that fit at 160 bytes each, the first list (and its advice) and the eight newest items are told, a note counting the lists left out between them. That sharing still cut choices: the end of a long list, the lists between, and a list past 2 KiB already when kept. **Since OWL-198 the choices are told typed, in a field of their own, rather than in a larger `result_refusal`:** Build's brief carries `refused_choices` (brief format 11), every kept choice, oldest first, so every choice reaches the run that asks, in one round for the decider; `result_refusal` keeps its 2 KiB cap for the runner's text, and tells the first list's full advice once, saying the choices are in `refused_choices` and how many were not kept, told whole, then the lists formats 6 to 8 kept as text and the latest reason, sharing the rest of the room as above (`BuildRefusal::told`). This reverses OWL-195's refusal of a brief format change ("Rejected" below): that change was a larger cap, which would still cut a long enough text, for a case that takes a person's restart at each step; a typed field ends the cutting of choices, keeps them apart from the advice they shared the room with, and gives OWL-193 a list to check a retry against, which a cap does not. "The refused choices" for a told run, OWL-193's check included, are those of `refused_choices`, plus those the lists formats 6 to 8 kept quote; a choice's place in `refused_choices`, from 1, stays the same for every run the hold tells, the list only growing until it clears, and is what such a check can match (OWL-193, below, defines the matching; text lists have no place, OWL-204 below says why they are left). A brief that carries a reason is told, so `stage_event` never frees its run's refusal (OWL-183), in a later command too.
- **Written when.** A refusal is written right after its run, before the state moves, so a park keeps it, and makes the ticket ref of a ticket that had none, a third exception to "makes no ticket ref until a round opens" after OWL-138's kept decision: `continue` then runs Build at Build, a PARKED comment says `continue`, and `do` starts from Ready keeping it. A clear is never written alone: a result that asks clears it with what keeps its questions, the round's ask or the resolver's first kept decision, so a QUESTIONS post that fails, or a resolver that breaks isolation, leaves it kept; any other clear goes with the next state kept. A write that fails stops the command. Watch continues through `OnDemand::continue_seen`, the same `continue` path.
- **What remains.** Under a hold the ticket cannot deliver until a Build run asks: a person who wants it delivered with a choice settled answers on the ticket and restarts it, and the run asks anyway, the decider confirming what the thread settles (OWL-186's false refusals, OWL-222); `owlshift forget` keeps the hold with the round count (OWL-202), and deleting the dedicated checkout drops it with every ticket's record. A run that asks some refused choices and drops others is refused since OWL-193 (below). Since OWL-198 every kept choice is told, within two bounds: a text past 512 bytes is told by its beginning, marked `[cut]`, and the role asks it as told; and a hold keeps 64 choices, the run being told how many more were listed and to ask about every choice its branch's commits took without the decider, since past 64 a run is not asking but dumping, and the ticket ref must stay readable (64 choices of two 512-byte texts stay far within its 1 MiB read cap, which `ticket_ref::write` does not check, even escaped as JSON). A choice listed again is kept again, as listed. Lists formats 6 to 8 kept as text still share the room as OWL-195 told them. Each told refusal costs a failed run and parks the ticket by the second, so a list past the first two needs a person's restart. `gate_failure` stays in the command's memory.
- **Rejected.** Clearing on any accepted result, as the ticket first put it: a `blocked` or `failed` run would drop the hold without asking, and the restart's run could end `done`. Keeping only the latest reason: the refused `done` that parks the told run says nothing of the choices, so the restart's run would be told to ask choices it is not shown. Writing a clear as soon as the asking run ended: a round whose post failed would leave the ref with neither the hold nor the questions. Keeping refusals of decisions only: the ticket asks for the reason too, and a refusal of any kind told across commands is never freed twice. Telling the latest list of choices instead of the first (OWL-194): it loses the first list from the second refusal on, where keeping the first loses a list only from the third, so the run would sooner be told to ask choices it is not shown, as with the latest reason above; and it needs the typed refusal kind too. For OWL-195: a flag saying whether the latest reason lists choices, beside an unchanged `reason`: two fields for one fact, where the reason kept apart from the lists says it. Keeping only each list's choices and telling the advice once: format 6 and 7 hold whole reasons, whose choices could be split off only by reading the text. Cutting the told text with its beginning kept, as before: a long first list hides every later one. Raising the cap: the brief's format would change for a case each step of which takes a person's restart (OWL-198 changed it for a typed field instead, "What the run is told" above). Refusing a format-6 or 7 Build refusal, as format 5's: a ref that held a hold would load without it, and the next run could end `done`. For OWL-198: telling within the cap what fits and keeping the rest held after the run that asks: it splits the clear that goes with the asking run's questions, and spreads the choices over rounds for the decider. Packing the choices compactly in `result_refusal`: no packing fits every choice of model-written text in 2 KiB. No bound on the kept choices: one refused result listing thousands could make the ticket ref too large to read. Keeping a re-listed question once: a cut can make two choices one, and a changed recorded text would be dropped; the decider is asked once either way.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/open_points_restart.toml` is the acceptance, on `open_points`' fixture: the first Build run decides both points and is refused; its told retry stops at a usage limit; after a `continue` step (a person's new command, which the stand-in driver refuses without a ticket ref and which restarts a parked ticket) the next run is still told and held, reports `done`, is refused and parks with a PARKED comment that says `continue`; after the restart, the next run is told the choices first and asks. `a_decisions_refusal_is_told_across_commands` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the ticket ref through `do`, `continue` and `do`: the ref made at the refusal, the hold through a usage limit and a park, the choices kept, a round whose post failed leaving it kept, and the run that asks clearing it. `a_build_refusal_is_kept_until_heeded` (`on_demand`) pins the rule for every kind of outcome, the lists kept through three refusals and a refused `done`, and a reason that listed none dropped by a later list. OWL-198's acceptance is `every_refused_choice_is_told_to_the_next_build_run` (`on_demand`), with results the executor refuses and the ref read back from its file after each: twelve refused lists then a refused `done`, one list longer than the whole cap, and a list past 64 choices tell every kept choice in `refused_choices`, oldest first, the advice once within the cap, and the count of those left out; `every_refused_choice_is_told_across_commands` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) plays three lists, one past the cap, through `do` and `continue`, and `open_points_restart.toml` checks `brief_refused_choices`. The contracts tests the format-5 to 8 reads, the bounds, the largest ref a hold makes, and `a_build_refusal_is_told_within_the_cap` the shares, the note past ten items and the advice never cut.

**A retry asks every refused choice** (OWL-193, decided 2026-10-08). OWL-186 refused a told Build run's `done` but read only the status: a retry that asked some refused choices and dropped others was accepted, its result cleared the hold (OWL-192), and the dropped choices stayed settled by the refused run's commits without a human. OWL-198 made the choices typed, each at a place in the brief's `refused_choices` that stays the same for every run the hold tells.

- **The rule.** A Build run told of refused choices names, when it asks, where it asks each one: `refused_choices_asked` (`result.json` format 7, so an older runner meets it with an "upgrade" error, not an unknown field), one entry per choice of its brief's `refused_choices`, in order, `{"choice": <its place, from 1>, "question": "<the id of a question of the same result>"}`. `RunResult::validate_against` refuses a result that asks under such a brief without naming every place exactly once, in order. The reason gives the instruction first, then the places not named (or repeated, out of order, past the count), then each unnamed choice's question cut to 80 bytes, so the 2 KiB cut of a kept reason keeps what the next run needs; the run has the choices whole in `refused_choices`. `RunResult::validate` refuses an entry naming a question the result does not ask, or the field in a result that asks nothing, and the schema carries that rule; `validate_against` refuses the field when the brief gives no refused choice, whatever the role.
- **What asks.** A result asks when it is `questions`, or `premise_false` with a question (`RunResult::asks`). A `premise_false` with no question asked nothing, yet cleared the hold (OWL-192): it now keeps it, as `blocked` and `failed` do (`on_demand::build_refusal_after`), so the Build run after the decider's answer is still told the choices. The role already allowed a held run to end `premise_false`, so refusing that result instead would have parked a legitimate stop.
- **The kind.** A plain refusal (`Failure::InvalidResult`), not a `DecisionsRefusal`: the hold lives in the ticket ref, and `build_refusal_after` keeps it, with its choices, through any refusal that lists none, keeping the reason as the latest one, told after the lists. A result that lists decisions is still refused for them first. Under a hold the brief carries `result_refusal`, so this refusal is a told run's and counts (OWL-183): at the stage's last attempt it parks, and the restart's run is told why.
- **How reliable.** The match is exact: a place and an id, both typed, with no reading of text. What it checks is what the run declares, not that the question named truly asks the choice: a run that asks only the first of two choices and names Q1 for both passes. The rule turns the failure it targets, a choice silently left out, into a refusal, and leaving one out on purpose into a false claim in the result. Several choices may name one question, and a choice split over several questions names one of them, so merging or splitting choices (why OWL-186 rejected a count) is not refused.
- **False refusals.** A retry that asks every choice but leaves the field out, or numbers it wrong, is refused, and counts: at the last attempt it parks, at the cost of a PARKED comment and a person's `continue`, whose run is told the reason. `roles/build.md` shows the field and says that a result that asks without naming every choice is refused; the reason names the field and the places.
- **What remains.** Holds that `questions.json` formats 6 to 8 kept as text have no place to name: a mapping is refused under a text-only hold, and their choices go unchecked, an exception OWL-204 accepts ("A hold kept as text" below). Choices past the 64 a hold keeps (`left_out`) are not in `refused_choices` and are not named; the run is told how many and to ask about every choice its commits took. The mapping is checked at validation; where it shows is the next point (OWL-203). Since OWL-222 the resolver never decides a question that asks a refused choice ("Past the resolver" below).
- **Where it shows** (OWL-203, decided 2026-10-11). The accepted mapping goes where the decider reads and where the run is audited, not into a format: `on_demand::asked_choices` pairs each place named with its choice from the brief, and the Writer shows each choice under the question that asks it. The resolver renumbers what it leaves for the decider (`resolver::left_for_decider`), so the round's mapping is translated to the round's ids where the round is built (`resolver::reopened_in`). The QUESTIONS comment says, once, that some questions reopen choices an earlier run made without the decider, which Owlshift refused, as the asking run lists them, and each such question carries, per choice, the choice's question and what the refused run recorded, flattened and whole (each at most 512 bytes). The event log keeps it: the `opened: questions` gate event gains `reopened`, `{"choice": <place>, "question": <round id>, "raised_as": <the run's id>}` per choice; the key is absent when the run was told no typed choice, so nothing changes without a hold or under a hold kept as text. The lines state the run's claim, not a checked fact ("How reliable" above): the decider reads the question to judge it. A RE-ASK does not repeat them, the round's QUESTIONS comment holding them; the late-comment round (OWL-139) asks the runner's own questions and carries none. Later runs are not told: the brief's thread holds each question and its answer, which is what they act on, and the hold is cleared once the round or a decision is kept. Rejected: keeping the mapping on the ask in `questions.json`, shown in later briefs' thread, a format change of the ticket ref and of the brief, refused by older runners, for a record no run reads, with places that mean nothing outside the told run (as the field on `Question` above); the event alone, which the decider never sees.
- **Past the resolver** (OWL-222, decided 2026-10-11), architecture section 4's "Resolver first". With OWL-203 alone, a question that reopens a refused choice still went through the resolver, routed by its category: a resolver decision on it was kept, cleared the hold, and settled the choice by a reversible DECISION, without the human whose absence the refusal was about. Now `resolver::route` sends every question the asking run names in `refused_choices_asked` to the decider, whatever its category, the run's other questions routed as before; `on_demand::resolve` and the stand-in driver pass it the run's reopened choices (`on_demand::asked_choices`). A run whose questions all reopen a choice, or are always-human, runs no resolver. The routing reads the run's typed mapping, so it holds as "How reliable" above says: a question named for a choice goes to the decider whole, other content included, and a choice asked under a question the run does not name is routed by that question's category. No DECISION can carry a reopened choice any more, since the resolver is never given its question and `resolver::settle` drops a resolution on a question it was not given; the DECISION comment's refused-choice lines and the `reopened` key of its `tracker_write` event, which OWL-203 added, are removed. A decision on another question of the same run still clears the hold, its round asking the reopened choices. Under a hold kept as text (OWL-204) no question is named, so nothing changes there. The cost is OWL-186's false refusals: a choice the thread, a rule or the repository settles since costs the decider a question rather than a resolver run, the run's `context` naming what settles it. `roles/build.md` says a question named in `refused_choices_asked` goes to the decider. Rejected: keeping the resolver on such a question, its decision reversible and shown (OWL-203), the settling without a human the refusal stopped, by another model; running the resolver and refusing its decision on such a question in `resolver::settle`, a run whose only outcome is the decider's question; a category for reopened questions, which the run writes and the runner could not check.
- **Rejected.** Matching the question text: a run writes each choice as a question that stands alone, so a copied text makes a worse question, a choice cut to 512 bytes cannot be copied whole, and any rewording would be refused. A field on each question naming its choice: `Question` is shared by the brief's thread, `questions.json`'s asks, the resolver's brief and the Writer's posts, so it would change three formats and carry places that mean nothing outside the told run. Question ids by position, without `choice`: one number fewer, but a refusal could then say only that the length is wrong, not which choice is missing, and a told run's refusal parks. Recording why not: since OWL-198 the place is typed and stable, so matching takes no judgment.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/open_points_partial.toml` is the acceptance, on `open_points`' fixture: the first Build run decides both points and is refused; its told retry asks only the first, naming it, and is refused with nothing posted, the ticket parking with a PARKED comment that names the choice not asked; after the restart, the run is told why, asks both, naming each, and round 1 opens. `refused_choices_asked_against_the_brief` (`owlshift-contracts`) pins each refusal, a merged mapping accepted, a brief with no typed choice (a hold kept as text) accepting an ask that names none and refusing a mapping, and the reason's head within the cap for 64 long choices; `result_schema_carries_the_expressible_rules` the schema rule; `a_build_refusal_is_kept_until_heeded` (`on_demand`) the hold kept by a `premise_false` with no question; `a_hold_kept_as_text_is_held_but_its_ask_unchecked` (`on_demand`) a hold kept as text, read from the ticket ref (OWL-204, below). The last step of `open_points_partial.toml` is OWL-222's acceptance: the told run asks both choices, one of them, where the README lists the example, under a category the resolver may decide and which the README settles, and round 1 opens without a resolver run, each choice under the round's question that asks it; `open_points.toml` and `open_points_restart.toml` open the same round. `a_reopened_choice_goes_to_the_decider` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) a run asking two reopened choices and a third question: only the third reaches the resolver, which decides it, its DECISION showing no choice, and the round renumbers the other two with their choices and the `opened` event; `a_question_that_reopens_a_refused_choice_goes_to_the_decider` (`resolver`) the routing and a resolution on such a question never logged; `reopened_choices_are_shown_as_the_run_lists_them` (`writer`) the lines.

**A hold kept as text** (OWL-204, decided 2026-10-09). OWL-193 checks a held retry's ask against the places of the brief's typed `refused_choices`. A hold that `questions.json` formats 6 to 8 kept as text (`BuildRefusal::lists`) has no place to name, so a retry under it that asks anything is accepted and clears the hold: a choice only that text quotes could go unasked and stay settled by the refused run's commits. This is an accepted exception to the rule that a refused choice is decided by a person, recorded here with why.

- **Where it comes from.** Formats 6 to 8 were written by `main` on 2026-10-08 only, from 08:39 (OWL-192, #158) to 18:11 +0200 (OWL-198, #165), with no release. The current runner keeps every new refused choice typed (`BuildRefusal::keep_choices`); `lists` holds what it read from such a document (`LegacyBuildRefusal::upgrade`, the format-8 read), written back as format 9 with it, so a text hold comes only from a runner built from `main` in that window, which would still write one if run today. A format-6 or 7 upgrade puts the latest reason after the held one in `lists`, whatever its kind, so a list may quote no choice.
- **The rule.** Under a hold that keeps lists, the Build run is held (`decisions_refused`, its `done` refused, OWL-186) and told the lists in `result_refusal`, sharing its 2 KiB as OWL-195 tells them, so a long list is cut and, past ten items, middle ones are left out (`BuildRefusal::told`). It names in `refused_choices_asked` only the typed choices of `refused_choices`: none under a text-only hold, where a non-empty mapping is refused; under a hold mixed with typed choices a later refusal kept, each of those, checked as OWL-193 says. An ask that passes clears the whole hold, lists included, once its round or decision is kept (`Prepared::heeded`); a write that fails leaves the hold, and the next command's ask is unchecked the same way. `roles/build.md` asks the run to ask the choices the reason quotes, and says they have no place.
- **Why left.** No such hold is known to exist. The maintainer's machine, the only known operator, holds four ticket refs, all last written on 2026-10-06 and 07, before format 6 existed, and its event log ends on 2026-10-07: no command ran under the formats that wrote text holds (checked 2026-10-09 by listing the refs' files and reading the log's dates, no content printed). A hold kept as text keeps today what OWL-186 gave every hold: the run cannot end `done`, is told the choices and must ask. Only the check that it asks each is missing, and every way to add it costs more than the case is worth.
- **Rejected.** Migrating the lists to typed choices: a list is the runner's reason text, the choices quoted within it, at most 2 KiB and possibly already cut, so splitting it means reading the text, rejected for OWL-195; one coarse typed choice per list, its text cut to 512 bytes, would put a list where `refused_choices` holds a question, lose its end, and still leave a choice within it unchecked. Refusing the ticket ref at read, or refusing to run Build under a text hold, with a message: Owlshift has no command to edit a hold, so the only way past either is deleting the ticket ref, which drops the hold with the asks, and the next run, untold, can end `done`, less than the hold kept today, the reason OWL-192 and OWL-195 kept reading formats 6 and 7 ("A Build refusal kept across commands" above); a command to settle or convert a hold is new surface for a case no ref holds. Refusing at read and removing `lists`, `LegacyBuildRefusal` and the sharing of text items (a format 10): less code, for the same loss. Refusing a held ask that names no typed choice unless the result acknowledges the text lists: a `result.json` format, schema and role change, and an acknowledgement checks no more than today's ask. A warning event when such an ask is accepted: code and an event shape for a case no ref holds; it is how to make one visible if a ref is ever found. Telling the role that these choices go unchecked: it would advertise the gap to the run it binds.
- **Tests.** `a_hold_kept_as_text_is_held_but_its_ask_unchecked` (`on_demand`) reads a format-8 `questions.json` holding one list, as the ticket ref keeps it, and runs the executor's own validation: the brief is held, given no place and told the list; a `done` is refused and the hold kept; an ask naming nothing is accepted and clears it; after a later refusal adds two typed choices, the brief gives those two places and tells the list after them, and an ask naming both clears the list too. The contracts tests pin the format-6 to 8 reads and, in `refused_choices_asked_against_the_brief`, the mapping refused when the brief gives no typed choice.

**A refused resolver result told to its replay** (OWL-191, decided 2026-10-08). OWL-184 left the resolver untold: its refused result falls back to the decider, who gets every question. But a fallback round whose QUESTIONS post failed, or whose keeping failed after the post, leaves the ticket at Build, and a later command's Build run may ask the same questions, which the resolver then settles again with nothing telling it why its result was refused, free to repeat the same mistake and cost the decider those questions anyway.

- **The rule.** The resolver run after a refused resolver result is told why, in its brief's `result_refusal` (brief format 10, which allows the field for `build`, `answer_check` and `resolver`), whatever command runs it. `resolver::refusal_after`, for `OnDemand::resolve` and the stand-in driver alike, after each resolver run that did not break isolation: a refusal `result_refusal` tells takes the kept one's place; an accepted `done` clears it; a usage limit, a run that failed with nothing told and an accepted `failed` leave it, so the next resolver run is told the same. Every round kept clears it with its ask (`on_demand::ask`), since its questions then reach the decider. `roles/resolver.md` tells the run not to repeat it, and that the earlier run may have been given other questions, so the ids the reason quotes may not be its own.
- **Where.** In the ticket ref, as a top-level field of `questions.json` (`TicketQuestions::resolver_refusal`, format 7). The refused run's questions never became an ask, so there is no ask to keep it on (OWL-184's place for the answer check's); OWL-184 turned down a top-level field for the answer check because every new ask would have to clear it, which is here the rule wanted: a round kept is what ends the case. `TicketQuestions::validate` refuses a kept reason that is blank or past `MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES`.
- **Written when.** Right after the resolver run, before any DECISION or round is posted, making the ticket ref of a ticket that had none, one more exception to "makes no ticket ref until a round opens" (`owlshift do` above): a refused result whose round is then kept costs two ref writes, the reason's and the round's, on purpose, since writing the reason only once a post failed would miss a round posted but not kept, the ref write itself having failed. A write that fails stops the command before any round is posted. The write carries the rest of the ticket ref as the command holds it (`Prepared::questions`), like every other write: Build's kept refusal stays until the round's ask or a kept decision clears it (OWL-192).
- **What remains.** A told run whose Build asked other questions is told a reason about a result on questions no longer before it; the role says so, and what the reason quotes is data. The pass limit (`MAX_RESOLVED_PASSES`) is reached only through accepted resolver results, which cleared any kept reason. Deleting the ticket ref drops it with the asks.
- **Rejected.** Leaving it, as OWL-184 did: a rare case, but the one where the same questions meet the resolver again. The reason in the command's memory: the replay is always a later command. Telling it only when the replayed questions match the refused run's: the refusals the runner tells are about the result's shape, which any later run can repeat, and matching would keep the questions too.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/resolver_replay.toml` is the acceptance, on `resolver`'s fixture: the resolver resolves a question it was not given and is refused, the tracker refuses the fallback round (`tracker_refuses_questions`), nothing is posted; after a `continue`, Build asks again, the resolver is told why and decides, and the next Build is told nothing and is done. `a_refused_resolver_result_is_told_to_the_replayed_round` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the ticket ref across two `do` commands, the reason kept with no round and cleared by the told run's decision, and a reason the locked ref cannot keep stopping the command with nothing posted. `a_refused_resolver_result_is_told_when_its_round_was_posted_but_not_kept` and `tests/scenarios/resolver_save_fails.toml` (`ticket_ref_refuses_round`) pin the other order (OWL-196): the round is posted, keeping it fails, and the replayed resolver run is still told the reason kept before the post. `a_refused_resolver_result_is_kept_until_one_is_accepted` (`resolver`) pins the rule for every kind of outcome; the contracts tests the format-6 read, the field refused in older formats, and the bounds.

**The GitHub forge adapter** (OWL-17), in `owlshift-adapters::forge`, pushes the ticket's branch, opens its pull request and reads its complete check set. It has no merge, close or approve operation.

- **What it relies on,** checked read-only on `pitchopp/owlshift` on 2026-09-28 with `gh api`. GraphQL's `statusCheckRollup.contexts` on the head commit lists the check runs of every app and the commit statuses (`CheckRun | StatusContext`) in one paginated connection with `totalCount` and `isRequired(pullRequestNumber:)`; paged two at a time, pull request #16 gave four contexts over two pages, the same four check runs as REST `commits/<sha>/check-runs`. `mergeable` is `MERGEABLE`, `CONFLICTING` or `UNKNOWN`; `mergeStateStatus` is `DIRTY`, `UNKNOWN`, `BLOCKED`, `BEHIND`, `UNSTABLE`, `HAS_HOOKS` or `CLEAN`. The check run statuses, conclusions and commit status states are the schema's enums, read by introspection. An unknown pull request answers HTTP 200 with an error of type `NOT_FOUND`; a rejected token answers HTTP 401 with a REST-shaped body, "Bad credentials". The repository is private on a free plan: reading branch protection or rulesets answers 403, and the branch is not protected, so no check there is required.
- **API and credential.** GitHub's GraphQL API, since one query gives every check, whether required, and the merge state; REST needs three endpoints paginated separately. The token lives in the system keychain (service `owlshift`, account `github`, per project since OWL-308, part K of "P5 · Quality pipeline"): a fine-grained token with pull requests (write), checks, commit statuses and metadata (read), or the output of `gh auth token`. Owlshift does not read `gh`'s own login. The push goes through the `git` CLI and the user's own git credentials. From P3, a fix run's brief also reads failed jobs' logs, which a fine-grained token gets with actions (read): a pending live check, OWL-112's first step ("Watch after delivery", above), still pending after OWL-112, whose repair stops before any run when the token is refused every log.
- **Push.** `git push --porcelain <remote> <commit>:refs/heads/<branch>` pushes exactly the verified commit, never a force. From P3, a push to a branch watch follows carries `--force-with-lease=refs/heads/<branch>:<pushed>`, and only a rebase run's push replaces history (OWL-208). The outcome comes from the exit status and the ref's porcelain flag (C3): created, fast-forward, up to date, or rejected; no flag for the branch is a failure, reported with the end of git's standard error, any credential in a URL removed. Tested against a local bare remote with the bench's hermetic git.
- **Complete.** Every context of the pull request's head commit, read to the last page. The list is refused rather than returned partial when a page does not advance, the count differs from `totalCount`, or the head moves while reading; and the head must be the commit the runner pushed.
- **Verdict.** Not open when the pull request is closed or merged; red when a check failed or the pull request conflicts; pending when a check is not finished (a check run not `COMPLETED`, a status `PENDING` or `EXPECTED`) or mergeability is `UNKNOWN`; no checks when there are none; green otherwise. A check passes on `SUCCESS`, `NEUTRAL` or `SKIPPED`; any other conclusion or state, including one GitHub adds later, is a failure that a human sees. Every check counts, required or not. `mergeStateStatus` is reported, not judged: `BLOCKED` and `BEHIND` depend on reviews and branch protection that Owlshift cannot satisfy by itself.
- **Known limit.** A required check that has not reported yet has no context, so it cannot be listed; `mergeStateStatus` then reads `BLOCKED`. Reading the required checks themselves needs admin rights or, on a private repository, a paid plan.
- **Opening.** `find_open_pull_request` looks for an open pull request from the branch of this repository into the base, so a resumed delivery does not open a second one; `open_pull_request` passes the runner's title and body verbatim. Draft pull requests are optional: a private repository on a free plan has none.
- **Tests.** Fixtures recorded read-only on `pitchopp/owlshift`, in `crates/owlshift-adapters/tests/fixtures/github/`; the answer to `createPullRequest` is made up in the schema's shape and flagged, since recording it would open a pull request. An ignored live test pushes, opens, finds and reads a pull request on a disposable repository named by `OWLSHIFT_LIVE_GITHUB`, then closes it and deletes the branch.
- **Checked live on 2026-10-10 for watch after delivery** (OWL-208). The calls were read-only REST GETs on `pitchopp/owlshift` from a Claude Code cloud session. Its proxy adds the session's own GitHub credential, a GitHub App user token, not the personal token Owlshift stores.
  - *A red pull request.* The first head of [#89](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/pull/89), `cb7963e`, holds four check runs (`commits/<sha>/check-runs`): `check (ubuntu-latest)` (id 110572493177) and the aggregate `ci` are `completed`/`failure`, the macOS and Windows legs `success`. The failed runs' annotations hold only "Process completed with exit code 101." and "… 1." (and a runner-image notice).
  - *Its log.* `actions/jobs/110572493177/logs` answered 302 to a signed URL on `productionresultssa17.blob.core.windows.net`, so a check run's id is its Actions job's. The log was 54,038 bytes and 708 lines. Its first `##[error]` line, the 692nd, followed clippy's `unused import` error, with post-job cleanup after it.
  - *What CI tested.* The run, `actions/runs/36922732108` with event `pull_request`, fetched `pull/89/merge` and logged "HEAD is now at e88d79f Merge cb7963e… into c0c0867…": the merge into the base of the time. `ci.yml` uses `pull_request`'s default activity types, so a later base move does not run it again.
  - *A check still running.* `main`'s head, `ddc6ff9`, had `check (windows-latest)` `in_progress` with conclusion `null`, under the same names as on pull requests, and no `ci` until the matrix ended. On #89, `ci` started 2 s after the last leg finished. The combined status of a commit without statuses answered `pending` with `total_count` 0.
  - *Mergeability.* Closed unmerged #141 to #145 still answered `mergeable: true`, `mergeable_state: clean`. Merged #89 and #175 answered `null` and `unknown`. At 14:58 UTC no pull request was open; #176 and #177 were opened later that day.
  - *Rate limits.* Every answer carried `X-RateLimit-Limit`, `-Remaining`, `-Used`, `-Resource` and `-Reset`. For this credential, `core` was 15,000 an hour and `graphql` 10,000. `remaining` fell by 49 between two calls a minute apart, spent by other sessions on the same credential.
  - *Not reachable from the session.* GraphQL answered 403, "GitHub GraphQL is not available from Claude Code sessions; use the REST API", and other repositories 403, "GitHub access to this repository is not enabled for this session".
  - *Documented, not observed.* No `pull_request` run while a pull request conflicts. Other apps' check output and commit statuses' descriptions, which this repository does not have. A rate-limited answer's shape: exhausting a budget to see it is not a check to run.
  - *Pending,* each the first step of its ticket ("Watch after delivery", above): (a) the checks query's GraphQL cost (OWL-209); (b) a real `CONFLICTING` read (OWL-209); (c) actions (read) for job logs (OWL-112), still pending after it (2026-10-11).
  - *`git merge-tree`.* Documented with `--write-tree`, `-z` and `--name-only` from v2.38.0, absent at v2.37.0 (`Documentation/git-merge-tree.txt` at the tags, read on git.kernel.org that day). Run with git 2.43.0 in a scratch repository: a content conflict and a modify/delete conflict exited 1 with the tree, the two paths and, under `-z`, the records `CONFLICT (contents)` and `CONFLICT (modify/delete)`; a clean merge exited 0 with the tree alone.

**The delivery report** (OWL-18), in `owlshift-runner::writer`, is the Writer's first write: a `[owlshift] DELIVERY` comment on the ticket once its pull request is open.

- **Content.** The pull request and its head commit; the complete check set (OWL-17) with its verdict, every check and the forge's merge state; the project gate, as a value OWL-16 fills (until then, "not run by Owlshift"); the decisions taken without a human; what remains for a human, derived from the rest: review and merge, unfinished or failing checks, a conflict, proposed follow-ups that nothing files yet. A check set that could not be read, or that belongs to another commit than the head, is said so; the report still goes out.
- **Writing.** The Writer asks the floor before posting. When the ticket's newest delivery report already had exactly the same body, it returned that comment instead of posting, so a retried delivery did not post twice. Since OWL-231 a DELIVERY comment already on the ticket naming the same pull request head, with the same lead line, counts as posted whatever its checks say (the OWL-218 follow-up of "Watch after delivery"). Text from a model or the forge is flattened to one line, and a check link is kept only for a plain `https://` URL, so neither can forge the header or the footer nor break the layout.
- **Live check, 2026-09-28.** `crates/owlshift-runner/tests/writer_live.rs`, run by hand, read the check set of [#25](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/pull/25) and posted its report on OWL-18 through the runner's own credential openers ([comment](https://linear.app/owlshift/issue/OWL-18/post-the-delivery-report-on-the-ticket#comment-f65e55c5)). Linear stored the body verbatim, the HTML-comment footer included, so `MarkedComment::parse` read it back with its footer, and the retry posted nothing. Whether Linear's editor shows the footer was not checked. This validates the ticket's acceptance before `owlshift do` exists; OWL-20 wires the Writer into it.

**`owlshift do`, `init` and `logs`** (OWL-20), in `owlshift-runner::{on_demand, project, events, init}`; the CLI only opens what they need.

- **Which checkout.** This settles the point OWL-15 left open (decided 2026-09-29). Worktrees hang off a dedicated clone of the project's `origin` under Owlshift's data directory, never the person's checkout, so a person editing or committing there does not look like a breach. The layout is `<data>/projects/github.com/<owner>/<repo>/`:
  - `checkout`: the clone, the runs' main checkout;
  - `worktrees/<ticket>`;
  - `runs/<ticket>/<run>` with `brief.json`, `stdout.log`, `stderr.log`, `gate.log`;
  - `lock`;
  - `unverified`.

  Names are lower-cased, and a Windows device name gets a trailing `_`. The first `do` clones; later ones set the clone's `origin` to the person's, `fetch --prune`, then `remote set-head origin --auto`, so a project that changed its default branch gets its new base; a failure of that call stops `do` rather than keep a stale base. The base is `origin/HEAD`, which a clone records; the pull request's base is that branch. Checked on 2026-09-29: git 2.54 sets `refs/remotes/origin/HEAD` to `origin/main` when cloning a local bare repository, and GitHub advertises the symref (`git ls-remote --symref origin HEAD` on this repository answered `ref: refs/heads/main`).
- **Refusals before anything runs.** A machine where agents cannot be confined (no `bwrap`, or native Windows), or no usable Claude Code token for agent runs in the keychain: what the executor would refuse at the spawn (OWL-41, OWL-94) is refused before anything is cloned, with the fix (`claude setup-token`, then `owlshift init`). On Linear, a ticket of another team than `tracker.team`: `owlshift do LOC-12` in an OWL project would otherwise build and report on another team's ticket. An `origin` that is not on github.com, or whose http(s) URL carries a user or a token (the URL is never repeated). A ticket with no decider (OWL-124, "Who answers which ask" above): before anything is cloned when it has no assignee and declares no zone, otherwise once the fetch gives the base whose `owlshift.toml` names the zone owners. Another `do` on the same project: an OS file lock (`File::try_lock`), released even by a crash. A missing keychain entry. A forge that refuses the token: one read of the pull request is made before the run, so a bad token fails in seconds, not after an hour of Build. The runner's own git drops the variables that point git at another repository (`GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE`, `GIT_INDEX_FILE` and the like), and keeps the person's authentication ones.
- **The run.**
  - The brief: the ticket, its thread, the gate and the project's rules (below). A comment is `owlshift` when it parses as a marked comment, which only ever demotes; `decider` when the decider's account wrote it (since OWL-124, the decider in force at its place in the thread: "Who answers which ask" above); `other` otherwise. The ticket's author is the account that created it (OWL-62: Linear's `Issue.creator`, the Markdown front matter's `author`): `decider` when it is the current decider's account, matched by identifier, so the description reads as instructions; `other` otherwise, and when the tracker names no account, so it reads as a request. On Linear, though, the author is never `decider` (OWL-74): Linear answers alike for an issue the decider typed and one created through their API key by a script or an agent, so the adapter never reports the creator as an account, and every Linear ticket reads as a request. Two limits are accepted, not worked around: an edit of the description by someone else after its creation is not seen, since the tracker names the creator only; and on Linear, a comment posted through the decider's own API key (by a script or an agent holding it) is the decider's. When the worktree holds `.owlshift/run/plan.md` and `ledger.json`, `checkpoint` points to them, so a fix run or a new `do` resumes from the plan.
  - The harness: Build runs on Claude Code with the `standard` tier's model, or the CLI's default, `network: false`, a 2-hour deadline and the gate's 30 minutes. The agent environment is `AgentEnv::from_runner` with the project's `stack.gate_env` (OWL-44) and the personal `allow_gate_env` (OWL-63), confined, with Claude Code on the token for agent runs, read from the keychain with the other secrets (OWL-94). The credential check covers `github.com`.
  - The loop follows the core machine on a `trivial` pipeline cut at Build. A failed run, a red gate included, gets one more run with `gate_failure` in its brief; a second failure parks, save a refused result (or artifact, OWL-184) of a run not told of one, which never spends the last attempt (OWL-183). `on_demand::stage_event` maps a run's outcome onto a core event for `do` and the scenario driver alike.
- **The project's rules** (OWL-61, decided 2026-09-29), in `owlshift-runner::rules`. Conventions such as DCO sign-off must reach the build role as instructions from the runner, not only through what the harness loads by itself from the worktree.
  - Source: the files `stack.rules` names (OWL-67, below), by default the `AGENTS.md` at the root of the repository, the cross-harness convention (Codex reads it natively; this repository's `CLAUDE.md` only imports it). `CLAUDE.md` is not read by default: it is Claude Code's own loading mechanism, and injecting it verbatim would pass an `@` import, not a rule.
  - Rule files (OWL-67, decided 2026-09-29). A project names them in `owlshift.toml`, next to `stack.gate_env`: `rules = ["AGENTS.md", ".claude/rules/testing.md"]`, paths from the repository's root with `/` between folders. Without the key, the rules are OWL-61's: the root `AGENTS.md`, and none when the base commit has no such file. With it, each named file gives one rule, in the order named, and a named file the base commit lacks refuses `do`, naming it: a rule dropped unseen is a changed instruction too. A blank named file still gives no rule, as it holds no instruction, and its path is then absent from the `dispatch` event. `rules = []` gives no rule. Each file is read as below, at most 32 files are named, and 64 KiB is the limit of all the rules together: the sum of the sizes git lists for them, checked before any is read, the refusal naming each file and its size. The brief's rules thus stay bounded however many files are named.
    - Where the list comes from: the `owlshift.toml` that `do` loads, the person's own checkout, the file `stack.gate` comes from, whose commands the runner already runs. An agent reaches that file only through a reviewed merge. The files' text, which becomes instructions, is read at the base commit (Trust, below).
    - Files only. A folder or a glob is not taken: the files of `.claude/rules/` are scoped to paths by their `paths:` front matter, which Owlshift would otherwise inject for the whole repository; a project names such a file only if it applies everywhere, and path-scoped rules come with zones (P2). The list stays in `owlshift.toml`, so what reaches an agent as an instruction is changed there, in review. An `@` import in a named file is passed as text, not followed: a project names the imported file instead.
    - No section is left out. A named file is taken whole, like any rule, since a cut rule is a changed instruction and a Markdown heading is no reliable boundary (a fenced block holds `#` lines too). Text meant only for interactive sessions, such as this repository's tracker section, goes in a file the project does not name.
    - The paths are checked when the file is loaded (`ProjectConfig::validate`): a path that is empty, starts with `/`, holds `\`, a NUL or another control character, or an empty, `.` or `..` part (so a trailing `/` too), a path named twice, compared as written, or more than 32 paths makes `owlshift.toml` invalid with `stack.rules: ` and the reason. At the base commit, a named path under a symbolic link or inside a submodule has no entry and is refused as missing; a folder is refused as not a regular file. Each file takes its own `git ls-tree`, never one for all: checked on 2026-09-29 with git 2.54, `git ls-tree HEAD -- docs docs/deep` lists `docs/deep` alone, so a folder named with one of its own paths would read as missing; `sub/deep/x.md`, with `sub` a symbolic link to `docs`, and `agents.md` for `AGENTS.md` under `core.ignorecase=true` list nothing.
  - Trust: the files are read at the base commit, never in the worktree. The ticket's branch is written by agents, so reading it there would let a run promote its own text to instructions for the next. The base is resolved once, by `sync_checkout` after the fetch, through its full name (`refs/remotes/origin/<branch>^{commit}`), so a local branch named `origin/<branch>` cannot shadow it, and a remote-tracking ref a run moved is reset by that fetch first; the ticket's new branch starts from that same commit (OWL-51). The rules therefore carry what the forge's default branch holds, the trust the project already gives its own merged code, and are read once per `do`: the fix run gets the same rules, a later `do` those of the base then.
  - Reading: `git ls-tree -z -l` at that commit gives the entry, then `git cat-file blob` its content, with no filter, text conversion or hook. No entry means no rule for the default `AGENTS.md`, and a refusal for a named file. The entry must be a regular file (mode `100644` or `100755`) of at most 64 KiB, in UTF-8; a leading byte-order mark is dropped and a blank file gives no rule. Anything else (a symbolic link, a folder, a submodule, a larger file, other bytes, a git failure) refuses `do` before the run, naming the file: a rule is never cut, since a cut rule is a changed instruction.
  - In the brief: one rule per file, `source` its path as named, `applies_to` empty, which means the whole repository; zones are the ones the ticket's `zone:` labels declare until intake declares them (OWL-129, above), and path-scoped rules come with intake. The `dispatch` event names the rules' sources.
  - With the harness: since OWL-53, Claude Code loads no `CLAUDE.md`, rule or settings file from the worktree. Whatever a harness loads by itself from the worktree comes from the ticket's branch, so the build role reads it as data; the brief is the source that counts. The role's limits prevail over any rule: this repository's `AGENTS.md` also tells an interactive session how to reach the tracker, which the build role refuses and the sandbox closes anyway.
- **Rules per role** (OWL-185, decided 2026-10-07). A project adds rules to one role without touching Owlshift's prompt for it. Its conventions for building, such as a frontend style or how migrations are written, need not reach the resolver, nor later design, verify or fix; and the override in `.owlshift/roles/` replaces a prompt whole, and with it the limits, the result contract and the routing of questions every run relies on.
  - Configuration: `[roles.<role>]` in `owlshift.toml`, with one key so far, `rules`, a list of files read as `stack.rules` reads its own (paths, files only, `@` imports passed as text, each file taken whole): `[roles.build] rules = ["docs/skills/frontend/SKILL.md"]`. A role is named as its prompt file is (`build`, `resolver`), so the table and the override in `.owlshift/roles/` name the same thing. Only a role whose brief carries rules takes the table: `build`, `resolver` and `intake` now, each P5 role (`design`, `design_review`, `verify`, `arbiter`) as it is built (OWL-302, OWL-303, OWL-304); `rebase` takes `stack.rules` but no table of its own, since the issue lists three roles. `answer_check` is refused: it classifies the decider's answers from the brief alone, and an instruction there could only sway a verdict. An unknown role is refused too, since a misspelt table would drop its rules unseen. Either makes `owlshift.toml` invalid with `roles.<role>.rules: ` and the reason. An older Owlshift refuses the unknown table, so a project that uses it raises its `requires` to the first version that reads it.
  - In the brief: the role's rules follow those of `stack.rules`, one rule per file in the order named, each with its `source`, so the role reads them as it reads the others; its prompt is unchanged and its limits prevail over any rule. `stack.rules` keeps its default, the root `AGENTS.md` when the key is left out, beside a role's rules. A path in both lists, compared as written and the default counting as `["AGENTS.md"]`, is refused, since the file would be given twice. The limits hold per brief: 32 files and 64 KiB for `stack.rules` and the role's together, checked before any is read, the refusal naming each file and its size. The files are read at the base commit like every rule (Trust, above), and the `dispatch` event names every source.
